# Jacob de Wit: Study Notes

**Site:** Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source -- one conservation page heads him "(1685-1754)" while its own body gives baptized 19 December 1695; the 1685 is a typo, confirmed by leg 1 from the page read end to end), Dutch Rococo painter and interior decorator; the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. Pupil of Albert van Spiers (age nine to thirteen) and of Jacob van Hal in Antwerp; Antwerp Guild of St Luke 1714 (1713 in one source); copied Rubens's 39 ceiling paintings for the Antwerp Jesuit church in red chalk, 1711-1722, and after lightning destroyed the originals on 18 July 1718 his copies became the historical record, engraved by his pupil Jan Punt and published in 1751; famous for door and ceiling paintings in Amsterdam canal houses (he lived on the Keizersgracht, numbers 383 and 385) and country houses on the Vecht; grisailles in grey-white faking stone relief, a genre that bears his name, the "witje" (Dutch for white, a play on his surname); Moses Choosing the Seventy Elders (1737, Amsterdam Town Hall, now the Royal Palace), Apotheosis of Aeneas for Pieter Pels (Herengracht 479), ceilings with Isaac de Moucheron; with Jan Maurits Quinkhard Amsterdam's highest-paid painter, 4,000 guilders a year in 1742. No words by de Wit survive in any source read, except short Dutch working inscriptions on his own drawings.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Wilhelm von Kobell study: Kobell learned the Dutch cattle piece by etching dead masters in aquatint and taught it to Munich, first as a copyist in the Mannheim and Dusseldorf galleries, then as a professor, then as the painter of Napoleon's battles; now meet the Amsterdam man who spent his youth re-drawing a dead Flemish master's ceilings in red chalk from below, and whose fake stone is the one trompe-l'oeil mechanism this chain has never studied. The doorway is the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam.

One-hundred-and-sixty-second doorway check in a row. D1 "Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (EN Wikipedia Jacob de Wit, 72 lines, read end to end by legs 1 and 3; Jeffares; Rijksmuseum). D2 "the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (the witje; the Amsterdam canal-house ceilings). D3 "Dutch Rococo painter and interior decorator" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (Web Gallery of Art: "much lighter than Rubens's, with a Rococo delicacy and charm"; Rijksmuseum: interior decorator). D4 "pupil of Albert van Spiers and Jacob van Hal" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (RKD via Wikipedia, read end to end). D5 "Antwerp Guild of St Luke 1714" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (1713 in SB Conservation; 1713-1714 in Onno; the RKD page itself was not read end to end by any leg, so the RKD's own wording is second-hand). D6 "copied Rubens's ceilings in the Carolus Borromeuskerk in watercolour 1711-1722, the record after lightning destroyed them in 1718 (engraved by his pupil Jan Punt, 1751)" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (the medium is red chalk, not watercolour alone -- Onno: copied "in red chalks"; the British Museum group is red chalk; the date span 1711-1722 conflicts with 1711-1712 in another source; the 36 drawings against 39 panels gap is unexplained; see C516). ## The life

Jacob de Wit was baptised in Amsterdam on 19 December 1695, the son of Christiaan de Wit and Annetje Slootmans, "born in the lower middle class of Amsterdam" (Onno van Seggelen). At age nine he became a pupil of the Amsterdam painter Albert van Spiers, a painter and decorator of Amsterdam canal houses who had lived in Rome, and stayed with him until age thirteen. Van Spiers had himself been a pupil of Gerard de Lairesse, so Lairesse's classicising theory reaches de Wit second-hand through this line.

About 1708 (Onno) or in 1710 (Rafael Valls; Onno's Jupiter page: "His parents decided in 1710 to move to Antwerp") he went to live with his uncle and godfather Jacob, who called himself Jacomo, a wealthy uncle and merchant and art dealer in Antwerp. In Antwerp he was apprenticed to the history painter Jacob van Hal -- "From 1709 to 1711 he was apprenticed to the historical painter Jacob van Hal, and from 1711 onwards he drew from live models at the Royal Antwerp Academy" (Onno). He attended the Royal Academy in Antwerp from 1711 to 1713, where he "honed his drawing skills and began his lifelong study of Peter Paul Rubens". In 1713 (SB Conservation) or 1714 (Wikipedia, citing the RKD) he joined the Antwerp Guild of St Luke as a master.

The Rubens ceiling copies are his earliest known work and the hinge of his life. Rubens had been contracted in 1620 for 39 ceiling pieces for the Jesuit church in Antwerp (now St. Carolus Borromeuskerk), delivered 1621, designs by Rubens, execution mostly by pupils including van Dyck, for 7,000 guilders. De Wit copied the designs "during 1711-1722 in red chalks" (Onno); the British Museum holds a group of 36 drawings after the panels. On 18 July 1718 lightning struck the church and all 39 paintings were lost in the fire. After the fire, de Wit's copies "became a historical document" (Wikipedia). His pupil Jan Punt engraved his sketches and published them in 1751 as De Plafonds of Gallery-stukken uit de Kerk der P.P. Jesuiten, te Antwerpen, geschilderd door P.P. Rubens, geteekend door J. de Wit, en op koper gebragt door Jan Punt, Amsterdam, 1751. A proposed journey to Rome was refused funding by his uncle, and in around 1715 he returned to Amsterdam.

He came home to a Catholic network. He "forged an important relationship with Father Aegidius de Glabbais, a Roman Catholic priest of the schuilkerk, or hidden church, of Moses and Aaron. In 1716 and over the next few years, he painted several works, including an altarpiece for the Moses and Aaron schuilkerk, an altarpiece titled Baptism of Christ for Het Hart schuilkerk, now Museum Ons' Lieve Heer op Solder, and a portrait of Father de Glabbais in 1718" (SB Conservation). He became court painter for the Moses and Aaron church, which until the 19th century "possessed the largest collection of paintings made by Jacob de Wit in the period 1710-1749", and "became the favorite painter of the Roman Catholic churches in Amsterdam" (Onno). "As a Catholic, De Wit initially received many commissions from Catholic patrons. He made altarpieces for clandestine Catholic churches and his first orders for decorating houses came from fellow believers. Other clients soon also found their way to De Wit" (Rijksmuseum). He married Cornelia Leonora van Neck in 1720, by the Onno account, and the couple remained childless; one low-quality gallery page states flatly "He never married" -- the contradiction is unresolved on the evidence read (see Disputes). He owned two houses on the Keizersgracht, numbers 383 and 385.

His first profane commissions came from the Cromhout family for their residence on Herengracht: "In 1718 he painted ten ceiling pieces for the residence of Jacob Cromhout. These paintings were strongly influenced by Rubens and Jordaens" (Onno). "From the 1720s onward, de Wit enjoyed a steady stream of commissions, public and private. He created many ceiling and wall paintings into which he incorporated grisaille" (SB Conservation). "Most of his commissions came from the province of Holland, primarily from wealthy patrons in Amsterdam, Rotterdam, Leiden, and also from the city of Utrecht" (Onno). "His fame was limited mainly to this market."

The commissions that define him: the Apotheosis of Aeneas for the Amsterdam merchant Pieter Pels (1668-1739), for the large side room of 479 Herengracht, designed 1723, with overdoors and a chimneypiece (Juno Asks Aeolus to Release the Winds; Venus Asks Jupiter to Protect Her Son Aeneas; Putti around an Altar) and, with Isaac de Moucheron painting the wall hangings, the fullest statement of the painted room; Jupiter, Disguised as Diana, Seducing the Nymph Callisto and its pendant Jupiter and Mnemosyne, 1727, for the Catholic merchant and banker Jan Baptist de Surmont, Lord of Vlooswijk, for his country seat Loenen aan de Vecht; the ensemble at 168 Herengracht with de Moucheron, 1729-34, "one of the best-preserved interiors with painted decorations dating from the first half of the eighteenth century" (Bulletin); the Assumption of Callisto ceiling at 479 Herengracht, 1731; and the civic work -- Moses Elects Seventy Elders, made between November 1735 and October 1737 for the Council Chamber of the Amsterdam Town Hall, now the Royal Palace, "one of thirteen paintings by the artist on Hebrew Bible themes" (Wikipedia), and the Alderman's Hall ceiling of the Old Town Hall of The Hague, 1738, framed by grisaille putti personifying Freedom, Industry, Temperance and Fortitude.

He was, by 1742, the best-paid painter in Amsterdam. "Together with the portraitist Jan Maurits Quinkhard, he was Amsterdam's highest paid painter. In 1742, his annual income was valued at 4000 guilders and he lived in style on Keizersgracht" (Rijksmuseum). He was "the highest paid painter in Amsterdam, and also enjoyed international renown" (same page). Late work runs to 1751: the Kassel Four Seasons cycle for Landgrave Wilhelm VIII, and Bacchus and Ceres in the Clouds for Huis Bosbeek, Heemstede. He died in Amsterdam on 12 November 1754, aged 58. His estate sale began 10 March 1755: "At the sale of the painter's estate in 1755, there were more than eighty oil sketches for ceiling paintings" (Bulletin). He was dubbed "the Titian of the Amstel" (Rafael Valls); the 1986 exhibition at the Royal Palace was titled Jacob de Wit, De Amsteltitiaan / The Titian of the Amstel. "Jacob de Wit was the outstanding Dutch decorative painter of the 18th century, active mainly in his native Amsterdam" (Web Gallery of Art). Over his lifetime he amassed a large art collection, "including numerous works by Rubens, some by van Dyck, and others by Dutch and Flemish contemporaries and old masters" (SB Conservation), and was "also an engraver and a noted collector of Old Master drawings" (Web Gallery of Art).

His pupils are disputed. Wikipedia (citing the RKD) lists Jan de Groot, Dionys van Nijmegen, Jan Punt, Pieter Tanje, and the brothers Frans and Jacob Xavery; Tako Hajo Jelgersma was his follower. Onno flatly contradicts this: "De Wit had no real pupils and the only two artists mentioned as having been taught some basics by him are Johannes de Groot and Gerrit Dadelbeek." Only Jan Punt is firmly attested in both traditions, by the 1751 engravings. No named pupil carried the ceiling-painting trade forward as a school; the documented influence line is narrow -- Jelgersma's grisailles in de Wit's manner and Jacob Xavery's feigned bas-reliefs.

## M1. Grisaille as fake carved stone ("witje")

Monochrome painting "in shades of white and grey to create the illusion of a marble sculpture in low relief" (Rijksmuseum). The genre is named after him: "He was even identified with this type of painting in such a way that the genre bears the name 'witje' (Dutch for white, a play on his surname)" (Rijksmuseum). General technique context (EN Wikipedia "Grisaille", read end to end): "a painting executed entirely in shades of black and grey or of another neutral greyish colour. It is particularly used in large decorative schemes in imitation of sculpture"; "imitation of sculpture was intended, as sculpture remained more expensive than a painting, even one by an acknowledged master." De Wit's subjects for it: "groups of children symbolizing the seasons or elements" (Rijksmuseum); putti "busy with attributes associated with, for example, a season or an element" (Rijksmuseum). The brown variant is brunaille: "a brunaille is a painting executed entirely or primarily in shades of brown" (Wikipedia "Grisaille"), and the Bulletin repeatedly calls the four corner seasons of the Aeneas/Callisto ceilings "in brunaille" -- so de Wit worked in both grey and brown monochrome, the brown keyed to the room's red and maroon woodwork (see M9). Observable evidence: the Rijksmuseum's Winter (1740) and Autumn (1740); the four-seasons cherub sets at Hinton Ampner and Kingston Lacy; The Comedy (1743, Mauritshuis) as a ceiling grisaille; and the late Kassel Four Seasons (1751), organised as niches with a stele in each, a bust on a plinth, and putti playing with the god's attributes -- the witje as painted architecture, not painted picture. **Takeaway:** one palette, two tones (grey stone, brown stone), and the picture pretends to be part of the wall, not a picture on it.

## M2. Painted light matched to the room's real light

"It was customary to attune the painted light to the natural light in the room for which the picture was intended. De Wit did this with great ingenuity. Depending on the distance to the light source, he allowed the contours of the shadows to flow softly or delineated them sharply" (Rijksmuseum). The Bulletin makes this a forensic tool: "As was usual at that time, De Wit coordinated the painted incidence of the light in his interior paintings with the direction of the natural light in a room. This is not the case in the ceiling painting of The Assumption of Callisto. The light in the room, seen from the corridor wall, comes from the left. On the painting, however, it appears to come from the upper right... It is virtually inconceivable that De Wit could have lit the principal composition incorrectly." The same test locates the three Russian overdoors: in Juno Asks Aeolus the light comes from upper right, so it hung above the door in the corridor wall; in Venus Asks Jupiter the light comes from upper left, so it faced it as a chimney-piece; in Putti around an Altar the light comes directly from the front, so it hung above the double doors. And in the 1733 Jupiter and Callisto drawing, "the greyhounds on the left and right now both look towards the centre, subtly guiding the spectator's attention" (Onno) -- gaze and light both steer the eye. A modern copyist had to "reverse the light and shade on the grisailles due to a different position of the light source" -- practical proof the shading is directional, not decorative. **Takeaway:** light direction is a signature, a placement key, and a forgery and swap detector all at once; shadow-edge softness is a function of distance from the (real) light source. This is the single most generative mechanism in the study.

## M3. Ceiling construction: compass, construction lines, scale marks

From the Bulletin's technical examination of the Apotheosis of Aeneas oil sketch (SK-A-4659): "Pinholes indicate that he used a compass to draw the curves around the central field. Construction lines in pencil, visible to the naked eye and on infrared photographs, mark important points in the architectural frame... At the top of the sketch, on the paint layer, there is a series of pencil marks that line up with the length of the central field. These may be scale marks that helped the artist transfer the composition to the ceiling. If each square represented one foot, the ceiling painting would be 566 centimetres across." (Note 4: "There are precisely twenty squares of 1.9 cm wide. One Amsterdam foot is 28.3 cm. 20 x 28.3 is 566 cm.") The finished ceiling is therefore built by measured transfer, not freehand enlargement. The central field is "contained in a frame full of ornaments, with the four seasons in brunaille in the corners" -- a fixed geometry: coloured centre plus monochrome corners plus carved frame with gilded inner edge. **Takeaway:** the oil sketch is a scaled engineering drawing; the ceiling is the sketch multiplied by a measured grid.

## M4. The oil-sketch (modello) workflow

"De Wit made oil sketches as an interim step between the initial design and the execution of the work to give the client an idea of the eventual result" (Bulletin). "On the design drawings and oil sketches that De Wit made preparatory to his paintings, he often noted information about his client, the year of production and the destination. These inscriptions now form an important source for research" (Rijksmuseum). "De Wit kept his oil sketches and often made notes on the back, recording information about the client, the year the work was done and the location" (Bulletin). At his estate sale in 1755 there were "more than eighty oil sketches for ceiling paintings". The chain is: annotated design drawing, then oil sketch shown to the client, then measured transfer to the ceiling (M3), then finished ensemble (M5). The Bulletin also notes he "broadly followed the painted modello" while changing details in execution -- the Callisto centre against the Aeneas sketch's corners; the lion's-head shield and the swan added to Putti around an Altar between drawing and painting. **Takeaway:** the modello is the contract and the blueprint in one object; revision happens between sketch and ceiling, not on the ceiling.

## M5. The painted room as a total ensemble

"De Wit's interior paintings were not independent pictures, but had a fixed place in the interior. They were intended for large compartments reserved in the panelling and were part of a decorative ensemble to which all the arts contributed" (Rijksmuseum). The standard kit: ceiling centrepiece, overdoors (bovendeurstukken), overmantel and chimney-pieces (schoorsteenstukken), wall panels, plus (in collaboration) painted wall hangings. The division of labour with Isaac de Moucheron is documented: "As a rule, De Wit took on the overdoors and the ceiling painting, and De Moucheron made the paintings for the walls" (Bulletin); the 168 Herengracht ensemble (1729-34) survives in situ as the model. Rooms were sold with their paintings as fixtures: the 1740 deed of sale for 479 Herengracht expressly includes "all the wall coverings, the paintings in all the ceilings, chimney breasts and above the doors, as well as the six paintings by Moucheron in the large side room, further all the fixed mirrors..." -- the artist's name in a deed of sale "is unusual and tells us how much value was attached to this room" (Bulletin). The room's social function is stated too: guests would "amuse themselves playing games and enjoying conversation... Surrounded by De Moucheron's painted wall hangings and gazing up at De Wit's heavenly universe, they could imagine themselves in another world, away from the hustle and bustle of the city." **Takeaway:** the unit of work is the room; a de Wit "painting" removed from its compartment is a fragment of a larger machine, which is why so many survive as orphans.

## M6. Copying as salvage: the Rubens ceiling record

De Wit copied Rubens's 39 Jesuit-church ceiling designs in red chalk (1711-1722), in di sotto in su -- figures seen from below. After the originals burned on 18 July 1718, the copies became the only record: "While in Antwerp, he made a series of watercolour sketches of the Rubens ceilings in the Carolus Borromeuskerk in Antwerp. After the church was struck by lightning in 1718 these became a historical document" (Wikipedia). He "copied his own set several times" (Onno) and made "a second, more-finished set that was later reproduced as engravings" (SB Conservation -- see Disputes). The finished publication: Jan Punt's engravings, Amsterdam 1751, three years before de Wit's death; Punt was his pupil. The British Museum group is 36 drawings, c. 335 x 394 mm each, catalogued as Corpus Rubenianum I copies (Rooses 1-36). The mechanism: a young painter's study exercise (copying a master's ceiling from below, in chalk, at small scale) becomes, by accident of fire, the authoritative visual record -- and is then industrialised into print by a pupil. Note also that Rubens's own ceiling was itself a workshop product ("the execution was done mostly by pupils, including Anthony van Dyck") -- de Wit copied a copy-system. And late in life he returned to the same panels: Rubens-copy drawings dated 1750 survive in the Courtauld and the Museum Plantin-Moretus, the year before Punt's publication. **Takeaway:** copying is preservation, not derivativeness; the copy outlives the original and becomes the original.

## M7. The putti motif cache

"The paintings De Wit made for above a door or chimney often feature chubby nude boys, or putti in Latin. They are busy with attributes associated with, for example, a season or an element. De Wit painted such themes so often that he could mine an established cache of motifs" (Rijksmuseum). The cache is also a commercial product in its own right: "De Wit was also well known for his beautiful, highly finished drawings commissioned by collectors. Especially sought-after were these representing putti and cherubs which were considered ideal" (Onno). Jeffares catalogues a run of such heads and seraphim in coloured chalks and pastel, several signed and dated (e.g. "JdWit/1738", "JdWit/1748", one dated 1733). The mechanism: a reusable, combinable vocabulary of small bodies plus attributes (season, element, sense, art) that can fill any compartment -- overdoor, corner, frieze -- in either colour or monochrome. The 1733 Jupiter and Callisto drawing shows the cache being recombined: "the putti more numerous" than in the 1727 painting, a stag-hunting scene added in the background, Diana's hunting equipment replacing foliage as repoussoir (Onno). The late Kassel cycle (1751) is the cache at export scale: the same seasons plus putti, in uniform sizes (216-217 x 145-146 cm), for a German landgrave. **Takeaway:** the putti are a typeface; the art is in the setting -- the recombination -- not in inventing a new letter each time.

## M8. Di sotto in su and the opened ceiling

The ceiling painting's job is to dissolve the ceiling: "With their illusionism, sweep and colour, De Wit's ceiling paintings are utterly convincing. The viewer is transported into the world of the gods, who float in the sky and repose on clouds" (Rijksmuseum). The Bulletin's description of entering the Pels room: "As soon as you enter this room, your gaze is led upwards. The ceiling opens up, and high above you, you see gods and other heavenly beings sitting on clouds and hovering in the air." The standard layout is an oval or rectangular centrepiece (Apollo enthroned with Minerva and the Nine Muses, 1731; Apollo plus nine muses with four grisailles of the elegy, comedy, pastoral song and epos around, Leiden Rapenburg 48) with monochrome corners. The overdoors use the same viewpoint: "the dimensions and the perspective, which is based on a viewpoint from below, confirm" their placement (Bulletin, on the Russian overdoors). The Rubens copies trained exactly this: "The figures seen from below, so-called 'di sotto in su'" (Onno). **Takeaway:** the composition only works from below, from a specific standing point in a specific room; the work is completed by the viewer's body position -- a spatial, not pictorial, contract.

## M9. Colour keyed to the room: monochrome corners against coloured centres and dead-head purple

The Pels room's corner seasons "were executed in a darker shade of purple than in the sketch. They would have formed an entity with the finish on the panelling" (Bulletin). The 168 Herengracht restoration reinstated "the original colour scheme, which included the pigment known as dead head purple (caput mortuum)"; "Red and maroon were in fashion in the first decades of the eighteenth century. The popular pigment dead head... could be red, maroon or purple depending on how it was burnt" (Bulletin). Furniture and upholstery were keyed to the same palette (walnut furniture, red-seated armchairs, "evidence of a desire for unity in colour"). The mechanism: the monochrome corner is not neutral -- its hue is tuned to the room's woodwork, so the "stone" reads as part of the architecture rather than as a picture hung on it. **Takeaway:** the witje's grey is never just grey; it is the room's grey, mixed to match the panelling, and the brown brunaille is the room's maroon.

## M10. Inscription as administration and evidence

De Wit annotated his own working drawings with client, year and destination, in Dutch, in his own hand: "voor d'Heer pieterpels in sijne Edl sijkaemer 1723" (Bulletin, fig. 4); "Vor de Heer Jonas Witsen / schepen / met Corikeuren getekent" on the back of the preparatory drawing for the Witsen Jupiter and Callisto (Onno, quoting the inscription; the drawing was in the Rudolf Collection, sold Christie's Amsterdam 12 November 1990, lot 178). He signed works "J de Wit" / "JdWit" / "Wit 1723" (SB Conservation on the Vanitas, signed and dated 1732; Bulletin on Venus Asks Jupiter, "signed and dated lower left: Wit 1723"). The inscriptions are the reason modern research can reconstruct lost rooms at all: "These inscriptions now form an important source for research" (Rijksmuseum). The mechanism: the annotation is part of the production system, not an afterthought -- a paper trail from sketch to room that outlives the room. **Takeaway:** the archive is the work's second body; without the inscriptions, the Aeneas and Callisto mix-up would be unsolvable.

## M11. Allegorical programmes tailored to the patron

De Wit's subjects are systems, not decoration: the Four Seasons, the Five Senses, the elements, the virtues (Freedom, Industry, Temperance, Fortitude at The Hague), the arts (Apollo plus nine muses; elegy, comedy, pastoral, epos). The programme is chosen to flatter or instruct the patron: the Apotheosis of Aeneas for Pieter Pels -- "Apotheoses were regarded as a reward for earthly endeavours... Aeneas could also have been an example for Pels. If Pels worked hard and refused to be beaten, he would be rewarded. De Wit's ceiling gave him a glimpse of the heaven that awaited him" (Bulletin). Moses Elects Seventy Elders maps the biblical council onto the 36 Amsterdam councillors of the Vroedschapskamer (Google Arts and Culture). The Putti around an Altar overdoor (1723) is read as "an allegory of Aeneas's destiny... Aeneas's piety and bravery, the prediction of the safe voyage and the foundation of the new state and, then, eternal fame", with shield (force of arms), laurel (eternal fame) and swan (auspices; Aeneas as a swan swimming from burning Troy to the Tiber) (Bulletin). The Ovidian seduction scenes (Jupiter and Callisto, Jupiter and Mnemosyne) were "a fashionable theme for interior decoration and decorative painting during the Rococo period all over Europe" (Onno). The Diana modello (1731, Fondation Custodia) reads the same way in reverse: chaste Diana as a fitting subject for the widowed Petronella de Neufville, "who at that time had yet to remarry". **Takeaway:** a small set of classical programmes, recombined per room and per patron, with the iconography doing social work -- self-image, civic legitimation, erotic charm. The subject is a portrait of the client in mythological dress.

## M12. Versioning and the afterlife of a composition

De Wit reworked his own compositions across years and media: the Jupiter and Callisto painted 1727 for de Surmont, then drawn again in 1733 for Witsen with a different composition (more putti, smaller eagle, stag hunt, Diana's equipment as repoussoir, Callisto resisting rather than yielding -- "adding psychological tension to this graceful drawing", Onno); the Rubens copies "copied... several times" (Onno); the Aeneas oil sketch reused as the modello for the Callisto ceiling's corners (the corner seasons "correspond to the corners in the oil sketch. Although De Wit changed details in the compositions, he broadly followed the painted modello", Bulletin). Others then copied him: "Izaak Schmidt (1740-1818) copied after our drawing" (Onno). And his own works were physically versioned by later owners: the 479 Herengracht ceiling is a composite hybrid (Callisto centre from 601 Keizersgracht plus original Aeneas corners), and the Aeneas centre was sold to Russia -- the ensemble as a system that later hands disassemble and recombine. The mechanism: a composition is not a single object but a version tree (sketch, then painting, then redrawn variant, then copy by another hand, then physical relocation), and the tree is traceable through inscriptions, light direction and dimensions. **Takeaway:** versioning is the oeuvre; the hybrid ceiling at 479 Herengracht is as characteristic of his afterlife as any intact room.

## Works

## W1. Thirty-six red-chalk drawings after Rubens's ceiling paintings for the Jesuit Church, Antwerp

Date 1711-1722 (copies made during and after the Antwerp stay) -- search-verified, Onno van Seggelen: "the designs Rubens had made for the Jesuits church Antwerp, which de Wit copied during 1711-1722 in red chalks". Other sources narrow it: NiceArtGallery says "around 1711-1712"; the Sotheby's-derived account says the first set was destroyed by fire in 1718 and de Wit made a second, more-finished set. Medium red chalk on paper, each sheet. Dimensions c. 335 x 394-398 mm per sheet -- reported-but-unverified (British Museum records via search snippets: St Michael 335 x 394 mm; St Gregory 335 x 397 mm; Coronation of the Virgin 335 x 394 mm; Abraham and Melchizedek 334 x 398 mm; the BM object pages returned only boilerplate when opened end to end). Current location British Museum, Prints and Drawings, registration numbers 1921,0411.36-53 (the group of 36, acquired 1921); other sheets of the set are dispersed. What it shows mechanically: the originals were Rubens's workshop ceiling paintings (39 panels) for the Jesuit church in Antwerp (now St Carolus Borromeus). De Wit copied them from below, in red chalk, as di sotto in su studies -- figures foreshortened as seen from underneath. The copies became the historical document: after the church was struck by lightning and the ceilings were lost in the fire of 1718, de Wit's drawings were the record. His pupil Jan Punt engraved them and published the book in 1751 (De Plafonds of Gallery-stukken uit de Kerk der P.P. Jesuiten, te Antwerpen, geschilderd door P.P. Rubens, geteekend door J. de Wit, en op koper gebragt door Jan Punt, Amsterdam, 1751) -- Wikipedia read end to end. The Corpus Rubenianum volume on the cycle (J. R. Martin, 1968) treats de Wit's drawings as catalogue entries 1-36. FLAG: 39 Rubens panels vs 36 de Wit drawings -- the 3-panel gap is unexplained in sources read; the date span (1711-12 vs 1711-22) and whether the surviving BM set is the first set, the second set, or a mix is not settled by any source read end to end.

## W2. Mucius Scaevola before Porsenna

Date most probably done while still in Antwerp, i.e. c. 1710-1714 (Galerie Lowet de Wotrenge, read end to end: "The present very early work - most probably done when the artist was still in Antwerp"). Medium NOT VERIFIED (dealer page gives no medium). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location Galerie Lowet de Wotrenge, Antwerp (dealer; on the market). What it shows mechanically: a history painting of the Roman virtue virtus -- Mucius thrusting his right hand into the sacrificial fire before King Porsenna (508 BC). The dealer text stresses it as a "very early work". Mechanically it is the pre-grisaille, pre-ceiling de Wit: a single-scene history piece in the Rubens and van Dyck manner he was absorbing in Antwerp, with the body-as-rhetoric (the hand in the fire as the picture's single gestural engine). It is the baseline against which the later decorative system can be measured: no illusionism, no architecture, just narrative bodies. FLAG: attribution rests on the dealer page alone; no museum record corroborates it; date is the dealer's "most probably".

## W3. Baptism of Christ in the Jordan

Date 1716 (search-verified, allpaintingsstore; also SB Conservation: altarpiece painted 1716 for the Het Hart schuilkerk). Medium oil on canvas -- search-verified -- BUT Wikipedia's major-works list calls the 1716 Baptism of Christ in the Jordan a "Chalk and pen drawing" (Wikipedia read end to end). Medium conflict -- FLAG. Dimensions 260 x 180 cm -- reported-but-unverified (search-verified only). Current location Amstelkring Museum, Amsterdam (Museum Ons' Lieve Heer op Solder) -- search-verified. What it shows mechanically: a large-scale altarpiece for a clandestine Catholic church (Het Hart schuilkerk, now Museum Ons' Lieve Heer op Solder), painted the year de Wit returned to Amsterdam. At 260 x 180 cm it is already monumental -- the young painter (age 20-21) working at altarpiece scale for his Catholic network (Father Aegidius de Glabbais of the Moses and Aaron schuilkerk was his key early contact). The most likely reconciliation of the medium conflict is that two objects exist: a chalk-and-pen study and the oil altarpiece. The mechanism here is patronage, not technique: the Catholic hidden-church network gave the 21-year-old his first large commissions, and the altarpiece format (single tall devotional image, legible from the nave floor) is the upright counterpart to the ceiling work that follows. FLAG: medium conflict (oil vs chalk-and-pen) unresolved; dimensions search-verified only; the Amstelkring location is stated by secondary art sites, not by a museum page read end to end.

## W4. Study for the Goddess Juno, holding a sceptre, seated on clouds

Date c. 1716-1718 (Sotheby's lot 113 catalogue note, read end to end: dated by Guus van den Hout "to very early in De Wit's career, circa 1716-1718, which is consistent with the date of the ceiling decoration (1717-1718)"). Medium black and red chalk and grey and reddish-brown wash, within two sets of black ink framing lines; signed in black chalk lower right "Jdwit", numbered verso "No 3" (Sotheby's read end to end). Dimensions 340 x 241 mm (13 3/8 x 9 1/2 in) -- VERIFIED (Sotheby's). Current location private collection (sold Sotheby's New York, Old Master Drawings, 2018, lot 113; ex Hans van Leeuwen collection, exhibited Rijksmuseum 1975-76, no. 142). What it shows mechanically: a design study for the ceiling decoration of the main room of Herengracht 366 (Cromhout house, Amsterdam), 1717-1718. Juno sits on clouds holding a sceptre, drawn in de Wit's characteristic black-and-red chalk with washes -- the two-chalk plus wash system that lets him model both flesh (red chalk) and shadow and cloud (black chalk, grey wash) on one sheet. The catalogue note adds a mechanism clue: van den Hout suggests the Juno was influenced by Divine Providence in Pietro da Cortona's Palazzo Barberini ceiling fresco, and de Wit is known to have owned Cornelis Bloemaert's set of prints recording that project. So the young de Wit was already working from engraved ceiling models (Cortona via Bloemaert prints) before he had ever seen a Roman ceiling -- print-mediated ceiling design, the same channel as the Rubens copies. FLAG: the Herengracht 366 connection is van den Hout's expert opinion ("he believes"), not a document; the subject identification (Juno) is likewise his.

## W5. Ten ceiling pieces for the residence of Jacob Cromhout, Herengracht 366

Date 1718 (search-verified, Onno van Seggelen: "In 1718 he painted ten ceiling pieces for the residence of Jacob Cromhout. These paintings were strongly influenced by Rubens and Jordaens."). Medium oil on canvas (ceiling pieces) -- search-verified, inferred from the form; no source read states it. Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location Herengracht 366, Amsterdam (Cromhout Houses; the Biblical Museum occupies 366 and 368 since 1975) -- search-verified; whether all ten pieces survive in situ is NOT VERIFIED. What it shows mechanically: de Wit's first profane (non-religious) commission, from the wealthy Cromhout family, and his first multi-piece ceiling ensemble: ten pieces for one residence. The Rubens and Jordaens influence named by the source is the mechanism -- heavy Flemish Baroque bodies carried into a Dutch canal-house ceiling only a year after the Juno study (W4). This is the commission that turned the Catholic altarpiece painter into the canal-house decorator: "From then on Catholics and non-Catholics competed to obtain his services" (Onno, search-verified). FLAG: no museum or monograph page read end to end confirms the ten pieces, their subjects, or their survival; the claim rests on a dealer page; the 1717-1718 ceiling decoration (W4) and the "ten ceiling pieces 1718" may be the same commission described twice -- possible double-counting (see C513).

## W6. Zephyr and Flora with Companions and Putti in the Clouds, design for a ceiling piece

Date 1725 (Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen object page, read end to end: "Creation date: in 1725"). Medium pen and brush and brown ink, brush and watercolours on paper -- VERIFIED (Boijmans). Dimensions 261 x 200 mm -- VERIFIED (Boijmans). Current location Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen, Rotterdam, acc. no. MB 1939/T 1 (PK), purchased 1939, in storage (Boijmans read end to end). What it shows mechanically: a finished design drawing for a ceiling piece in the house of Cornelis Wittert van Valkenburgh on the Delftse Vaart, Rotterdam -- i.e. de Wit working outside Amsterdam by 1725. The medium (brown ink plus watercolour) is the coloured-design stage between the black and red chalk sketch and the oil modello: colour is now being tested on paper before it goes on the ceiling. Zephyr and Flora -- wind god and flower goddess with putti in clouds -- is the light, amorous Rococo subject that will recur for 25 years. Note the Boijmans artist line gives "Amsterdam 1695/1696 - Amsterdam 1754" -- a 1695/1696 birth-year wobble, FLAG (baptism was 19 December 1695; the "/1696" reflects the old-style year boundary, not a second date). FLAG: the executed ceiling for Wittert van Valkenburgh is not traced in any source read; only the design survives.

## W7. The Apotheosis of Aeneas, oil sketch (design for a ceiling painting)

Date in or shortly before 1723 (Rijksmuseum Bulletin, read end to end: "in or shortly before 1723"). Medium oil on canvas (Bulletin read end to end). Dimensions 51.5 x 69.5 cm -- VERIFIED (Bulletin). Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam, inv. no. SK-A-4659 (Bulletin read end to end). What it shows mechanically: the single most mechanically revealing work in this study, because the Bulletin article (de Fouw and Verslype) dissects how it was made and used. Venus anoints her son Aeneas with divine fragrance, elevating him to the gods; Eternity holds the ouroboros above his head; Mercury supports him; the Three Graces and Fame gather behind Venus; Jupiter sits centre-sky; Apollo, Minerva and an unidentified goddess watch from a cloud; the river god Numicius (who washed away Aeneas's mortality) below. The composition sits in an ornamental frame with the four seasons in brunaille (brown grisaille) in the corners -- Flora and spring, Ceres and summer, Bacchus and autumn, Momus and winter. Making: de Wit made oil sketches as an interim step between design drawing and execution, to show the client the eventual result. Pinholes show he used a compass to draw the curves around the central field; construction lines in pencil, visible to the naked eye and on infrared photographs, mark important points in the architectural frame; a series of pencil marks along the top of the paint layer are scale marks for transferring the composition to the ceiling -- twenty squares of 1.9 cm, one Amsterdam foot (28.3 cm) per square, giving a ceiling 566 cm across. De Wit kept his oil sketches and often annotated the backs with client, year and destination (this sketch has no visible inscription; it was lined). At his estate sale in 1755 there were more than eighty oil sketches for ceiling paintings. Light logic: de Wit coordinated the painted incidence of light with the natural light in the room -- the diagnostic test the Bulletin uses to prove the present ceiling in the room is a different painting (see W15). This is a portable mechanism: a de Wit ceiling is not a picture hung overhead; it is a light-calibrated extension of a specific room. Commission: Pieter Pels (1668-1739), for the large side room ("red side room") of 479 Herengracht, Amsterdam (Bulletin read end to end). The finished ceiling was later sold to a Russian grand duke (see W8-W10) and its present whereabouts are unknown -- "probably rolled up somewhere in the depot of a Russian museum" (Bulletin read end to end).

## W8. Juno Asks Aeolus to Release the Winds

Date 1723 (Bulletin, read end to end, fig. 11 caption; also "signed and dated lower left: Wit 1723" is given for the companion Venus, and the three 1723 paintings form one ensemble). Medium oil on canvas (Bulletin read end to end). Dimensions 116.5 x 80 cm -- VERIFIED (Bulletin). Current location Alupka Palace and Park Museum-Reserve, Alupka (Crimea), inv. no. zh-453 (Bulletin read end to end). What it shows mechanically: one of three overdoor and chimneypiece paintings from the Pels ensemble (479 Herengracht), traced to Russia via de Wit's three design drawings. Juno asks Aeolus, guardian of the winds, to raise a storm against Aeneas (Aeneid I). The Bulletin's forensic key: the painted light comes from the upper right, which indicates this piece hung above the then-door in the corridor wall; impressions of the strainer and cuts in the upper corners show it had an arch-shaped frame in the panelling. The three drawings' dimensions and their perspective -- based on a viewpoint from below -- confirm they are designs for overdoors and a chimneypiece, not easel pictures. Mechanism: light direction as placement code -- each overdoor is painted for one wall and one window, and the light direction is the signature that lets a displaced painting be re-hung in its original position a century later. FLAG: the Bulletin's dating rests on the design drawings (one annotated "voor d'Heer pieter pels in sijne Edl sijkaemer 1723") and stylistic kinship; the Alupka paintings hang as anonymous 18th-century works and were identified by the Bulletin's authors, not by the holding museum.

## W9. Venus Asks Jupiter to Protect Her Son Aeneas

Date 1723 (Bulletin, read end to end, fig. 12 caption; "De Wit's signature and the date 1723 can just be made out in the lower left corner"). Medium oil on canvas (Bulletin read end to end). Dimensions 116 x 96 cm -- VERIFIED (Bulletin). Current location Alupka Palace and Park Museum-Reserve, Alupka (Crimea), inv. no. zh-520 (Bulletin read end to end). What it shows mechanically: the pendant to W8 in the Pels ensemble: Venus kneels before Jupiter to beg protection for Aeneas (the episode following Juno's storm). Painted light comes from the upper left -- the opposite direction to W8 -- so this was the chimneypiece (possibly above the mirror named in the 1740 inventory) on the opposite wall. The pair (hindrance by Juno, help by Venus) flanks the ceiling's apotheosis: the room's whole iconography is a single Aeneas argument -- hindrance, help, triumph. The Bulletin notes the three Alupka and St Petersburg paintings were "three previously unknown paintings... added to the artist's oeuvre" by this research (Bulletin read end to end). FLAG: same identification caveat as W8 (Bulletin authors' attribution, holding museum unaware).

## W10. Putti around an Altar

Date 1723 (Bulletin, read end to end, fig. 13 caption). Medium oil on canvas (Bulletin read end to end). Dimensions 84 x 181 cm -- VERIFIED (Bulletin). Current location St Petersburg, Scientific-Research Museum of the Russian Academy of Arts, inv. no. zh-174 (Bulletin read end to end). What it shows mechanically: the horizontal overdoor of the Pels ensemble, originally above the double doors between the front and back rooms (confirmed by the painted light coming directly from the front). Putti around an altar with a holy flame -- an allegory of Aeneas's destiny: the altar is the fatherland (patriotism and piety), the shield (with a lion's head in the executed version, changed from the drawing) is foundation by force of arms, the laurel is eternal fame, and the swan above is the auspices -- twelve swans proclaiming Aeneas's safe passage, and Aeneas himself as a swan swimming from burning Troy to the Tiber, where Romulus and Remus stand for Rome's foundation. The executed painting differs from the design drawing (lion's-head shield added, swan added) -- evidence that de Wit revised iconography between drawing and painting, tightening the allegory. Mechanism: the overdoor as a compressed emblem -- one horizontal field carrying four symbols (altar, shield, laurel, swan) that together state the room's thesis, readable in a glance from below. FLAG: same identification caveat as W8 and W9.

## W11. Holy Family and Trinity (also: The Return from Egypt)

Date 1726 (Wikipedia major works, read end to end; search-verified, ArtsDot). Medium oil on canvas (search-verified). Dimensions 140 x 105 cm (search-verified, ArtsDot) -- reported-but-unverified. Current location Amstelkring Museum, Amsterdam (search-verified). What it shows mechanically: a Catholic devotional picture -- the Holy Family with the Trinity (God the Father, Christ, the dove) -- painted in the same year as W12 for the same milieu. Mechanically it is the upright, easel-scale counterpart to the ceilings: a devotional image built from the same Rubens-derived body language (heavy drapery, upward gaze, cloud-borne figures), but composed for eye level rather than from below. The 1726 pair (W11, W12) shows de Wit still serving his Catholic patrons with altarpiece-scale religious painting a decade after the Baptism (W3), even as the canal-house ceilings were becoming his main income. FLAG: dimensions and location search-verified only; no museum page read end to end.

## W12. Adoration of the Shepherds

Date 1726 (Wikipedia major works, read end to end; search-verified, topimpressionists). Medium oil on canvas (search-verified). Dimensions 138 x 103 cm (search-verified, topimpressionists) -- reported-but-unverified. Current location Amstelkring Museum, Amsterdam (search-verified). What it shows mechanically: the companion 1726 religious piece: shepherds adoring the Christ child. Nearly the same dimensions as W11 (138 x 103 vs 140 x 105 cm), suggesting a matched pair for one interior. The mechanism is the same as W11 -- Rubensian devotional bodies at eye level -- and the pairing itself is a mechanism worth noting: de Wit thought in pendants and ensembles even for easel pictures, not just ceilings. FLAG: dimensions and location search-verified only; the "pair" reading is leg 2's inference from the near-identical dimensions and shared date and location, not a sourced claim.

## W13. Jupiter, Disguised as Diana, Seducing the Nymph Callisto

Date 1727 (Onno van Seggelen, read end to end). Medium oil on canvas (Onno van Seggelen, read end to end). Dimensions 240 x 205 cm -- VERIFIED (Onno van Seggelen, citing inv. no. SK-A-3885). Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam, inv. no. SK-A-3885 (Onno van Seggelen, read end to end). What it shows mechanically: wall decoration commissioned in 1727 by the wealthy Catholic merchant-banker Jan Baptist de Surmont, Lord of Vlooswijk, for his country seat Loenen aan de Vecht, together with its pendant Jupiter, Disguised as a Shepherd, Seducing Mnemosyne, the Goddess of Memory (W14). Jupiter, disguised as Diana, bends over Callisto (Ovid, Metamorphoses 2:442-453; Fasti 2:155-192); the eagle symbolises Jupiter; putti, red drapery, rocks and trees fill the decorative field. The dealer's page compares the 1733 finished drawing of the same subject (commissioned by Jonas Witsen) with the 1727 painting: in the drawing Callisto doubts and resists, the eagle is smaller, the putti more numerous, a stag-hunt (the true Diana) appears in the background, the greyhounds look to the centre, and Diana's hunting equipment replaces the painting's modest foliage as repoussoir (Onno, read end to end). That comparison is a mechanism lesson in itself: de Wit re-composed the same subject six years later by changing the psychology (resistance instead of surrender) and the framing devices (dogs, equipment, background hunt), while keeping the central bend-over gesture. Also: the subject -- Jupiter's seductions in animal and disguised form -- "was a fashionable theme for interior decoration... during the Rococo period all over Europe" (Onno, read end to end), and the dealer notes de Wit's style evolving toward "light grace, easy movement and fresh colors in contrast to the former heavy baroque compositions" -- the Rococo turn, dated here to 1727-1733. FLAG: the painting's 1727 date and Surmont commission come from the dealer page (read end to end) with a footnote to the Rijksmuseum object; no Rijksmuseum object page was read end to end in this leg.

## W14. Jupiter, Disguised as a Shepherd, Seducing Mnemosyne, the Goddess of Memory

Date 1727 (Rijksmuseum story caption, read end to end: "Jupiter, Disguised as a Shepherd, Seducing Mnemosyne, the Goddess of Memory Jacob de Wit (1695-1754), oil on canvas, 1727"). Medium oil on canvas (Rijksmuseum story, read end to end). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (Rijksmuseum story, read end to end; Onno van Seggelen: the pair is "now in the Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam"). What it shows mechanically: the pendant to W13, same commission (Surmont, Loenen aan de Vecht, 1727). Jupiter as a shepherd seducing Mnemosyne, goddess of memory and mother of the Muses. The pairing logic: two Jupiter seductions, two disguises (Diana, shepherd), two nymphs and goddesses -- a symmetrical wall ensemble for a country house, the decorative equivalent of a rhyming couplet. The Rijksmuseum story uses it to illustrate "the ideal of the ensemble": these were not independent pictures but had a fixed place in the interior, part of a decorative ensemble to which all the arts contributed (Rijksmuseum story, read end to end). A finished drawing of this subject (pen, brown and black ink, washes, watercolour, heightened with white, 449 x 310 mm) is in the Art Institute of Chicago, inv. 2013.1047 (Onno van Seggelen footnote, read end to end). FLAG: dimensions not verified; the Rijksmuseum inventory number was not stated in any source read.

## W15. The Assumption of Callisto, the Four Seasons in the Corners (ceiling, 479 Herengracht)

Date 1731 (Bulletin, read end to end, fig. 8 caption). Medium oil on canvas (Bulletin read end to end). Dimensions: central middle section 360 x 535 cm; whole ceiling 850 x 530 cm -- VERIFIED (Bulletin). Current location 479 Herengracht, Amsterdam (large side room), private collection (Bulletin read end to end). What it shows mechanically: the ceiling that now hangs in the Pels room -- and the proof that the room is a composite. Callisto and her son Arcas are conveyed to heaven as the Great and Little Bear (Ovid, Metamorphoses II); the four seasons appear in the corners, matching the corners of the Aeneas oil sketch (W7) almost exactly (Bulletin fig. 9 compares them season by season). The detective story: in the 1880s-90s owner Cornelis van Mourik sold the Aeneas ceiling and overdoors to a Russian grand duke, then replaced the centre with this Callisto ceiling taken from 601 Keizersgracht (the house of Jacob Reijnst, who moved in 1713; the Hollenkamp clothes shop stood there when the ceiling was removed in 1886-87). The four corner seasons were whitewashed over and hidden, so they were not sold; the next owner, Willem Frederik van Leeuwen, had them cleaned and restored in 1897 and the carved oak frame brought back to its original state. The Bulletin proves the swap by the light test: the room's natural light comes from the left (from the corridor wall), but the Callisto painting is lit from the upper right -- "virtually inconceivable that De Wit could have lit the principal composition incorrectly" -- while the four seasons' lighting does match the room (Bulletin read end to end). The Callisto centre is also 30 cm shorter than the Aeneas centre (536 vs 566 cm), and the frame is closer to the Aeneas sketch's frame than to a Callisto frame. Mechanism: the ceiling as a movable panel -- de Wit's ceilings were painted on canvas and set into frames, so a 19th-century owner could unhook one masterpiece and slot in another, and the room's light-logic is the only witness that testifies. Also: the corner seasons (brunaille and grisailles, darker purple shade than the sketch) were executed to harmonise with the room's dead-head purple panelling -- colour keyed to the room, not to the picture. FLAG: the 1731 date is the Bulletin's caption; the Keizersgracht-601 provenance rests on Backer's 1897 memorandum (a unique, single source). The present private-collection status means no museum record exists.

## W16. Diana and her companions returning from the hunt (?)

Date 1731, signed and dated (search-verified, Fondation Custodia). Medium oil on canvas (search-verified). Dimensions 49.5 x 62.8 cm (search-verified, Fondation Custodia) -- reported-but-unverified. Current location Fondation Custodia (Collection Frits Lugt), Paris, inv. 5505, acquired 1938 (search-verified). What it shows mechanically: a modello (small oil sketch) for an existing ceiling in one of the finest houses on the Herengracht. The subject is read two ways: the reception of Diana's companion Callisto in heaven -- but de Wit painted that subject "far less ambiguously on a ceiling two houses along" in the same year (i.e. W15, 479 Herengracht), so the attributes held up by putti, including a hunting horn, make it more likely the goddess of the hunt herself is centre stage. Client: Petronella de Neufville, "who at that time had yet to remarry" -- chaste Diana as a fitting subject for a widow (search-verified). Mechanism: the modello as a client-facing miniature -- the same oil-sketch practice as W7, but here the sketch survives while the ceiling survives too, letting the two be compared; and subject chosen for the patron's biography (widow, then chaste Diana), the same personal-iconography logic as Pels choosing Aeneas (the self-made man rewarded in heaven). FLAG: subject identification is explicitly uncertain ("(?)"); dimensions and acquisition details search-verified only.

## W17. Moses Elects Seventy Elders (Moses Choosing the Seventy Elders)

Date made between November 1735 and October 1737; completed December 1737 (Google Arts and Culture, read end to end: "De Wit made this painting between November 1735 and October 1737 in response to a commission from the city fathers"; search-verified, Wikipedia: "completed in December 1737"). Medium oil on canvas (Google Arts and Culture, read end to end; search-verified, Wikipedia). Dimensions 5.940 x 13.710 m (5.9 x 13.7 m) (search-verified, Wikipedia and Commons) -- reported-but-unverified by an end-to-end museum read (the Google Arts and Culture page read end to end gives no dimensions; the Rijksmuseum story says only "over 5 by 12.5 metres", read end to end, a rounded figure that conflicts with 5.94 x 13.71 -- FLAG). One of the largest paintings in the Netherlands (Rijksmuseum story, read end to end). Current location Royal Palace of Amsterdam (former Town Hall, Dam Square), Moses Chamber (Vroedschapskamer, Council Chamber) (Google Arts and Culture, read end to end; search-verified, Commons: accession 12.01). Commissioned 1735 by the Amsterdam city council for the Council Chamber; part of a scheme of thirteen paintings by de Wit on Hebrew Bible themes, plus some 13 grisaille paintings of Hebrew Bible subjects in the same building (search-verified, SB Conservation; search-verified, Wikipedia: "one of thirteen paintings by the artist on Hebrew Bible themes"). What it shows mechanically: Moses stands centre, surrounded by the seventy elders he has selected (Numbers 11:16-17, 24-25); behind him, screened by a tent flap, the Ark of the Covenant with the Ten Commandments; a group of clouds top-left indicates God's presence (Google Arts and Culture, read end to end). The story illustrates the task of the 36 members of the city council who met in the chamber -- the biblical elders as a mirror of the Amsterdam vroedschap, advising the four burgomasters (Google Arts and Culture, read end to end). Preparatory work: several small-scale representations, including a gouache-on-paper sketch (28.6 x 45.8 cm, Agnes Etherington Art Centre, acc. 37-092, dated 1735, search-verified) that presents the work as a tapestry, differing significantly from the executed mural; oil sketches in the Musee de Picardie, Amiens, and the Bijbels Museum, Amsterdam (search-verified, Agnes). Mechanism: the civic mirror -- a biblical scene scaled to fill an entire wall (nearly 6 x 14 m) so the councillors sit inside their own typology; and the modello as a format test -- de Wit tried the composition as a tapestry before committing to the wall. FLAG: dimension conflict (Rijksmuseum story "over 5 by 12.5 m" vs Wikipedia and Commons 5.94 x 13.71 m); the precise figure is search-verified only. The "13 grisaille paintings of Hebrew Bible subjects" in the Town Hall is stated by one secondary source (SB Conservation) and not confirmed elsewhere in this leg.

## W18. Ceiling painting and grisaille corner putti, Alderman's Hall, Old Town Hall, The Hague (with the overdoor Audi alteram partem)

Date 1738 (search-verified, SB Conservation: "In 1738 he decorated the Alderman's Hall of the Old Town Hall of The Hague with a ceiling painting framed by allegorical putti (Freedom, Industry, Temperance, and Fortitude) done in grisaille in the four corners"). Medium oil on canvas (ceiling) with grisaille corner panels (search-verified). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location Old Town Hall (Oude Stadhuis), Groenmarkt, The Hague -- in situ (search-verified; Wikipedia Old City Hall (The Hague) confirms the building and lists "Overdoor by Jacob de Wit illustrating Audi alteram partem" among its interior works -- search-verified). What it shows mechanically: a civic ceiling in the same mode as W17 but a year later and in another city: a central coloured ceiling painting framed by four grisaille putti personifying Freedom, Industry, Temperance and Fortitude -- the four virtues of aldermanic government, painted as fake stone reliefs in the corners. The overdoor Audi alteram partem ("hear the other side") is the legal maxim of the room, set above the door as a motto-picture. Mechanism: grisaille as the moral frame -- colour for the heavens in the middle, monochrome stone-look virtues at the corners, so the allegory reads as architecture (the virtues hold the ceiling up, visually) while the gods float free. This is the witje used structurally, not decoratively. FLAG: no museum or municipal page read end to end; the 1738 date, the four virtue names and the grisaille medium all rest on the SB Conservation article (a conservation firm's collection page). The overdoor's date is not stated -- it may or may not be 1738.

## W19. Winter and Autumn (grisaille pair, the Rijksmuseum "witjes")

Date 1740 (Rijksmuseum story captions, read end to end: "Winter Jacob de Wit (1695-1754), oil on canvas, 1740" and "Autumn Jacob de Wit (1695-1754), oil on canvas, 1740"). Medium oil on canvas, grisaille (Rijksmuseum story, read end to end: the "Namesake of a genre" and "Putti" sections describe these as monochrome paintings in shades of white and grey creating the illusion of marble sculpture in low relief, often groups of children symbolising the seasons). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED. Current location Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam (the story illustrates them from the collection; no inventory numbers given in the story) -- location inferred from the story context, FLAG. What it shows mechanically: the mature witje at its most standardised: putti (chubby nude boys) busy with the attributes of a season -- the motif de Wit painted "so often that he could mine an established cache of motifs" (Rijksmuseum story, read end to end). The Rijksmuseum story's key technical passage belongs here: "It was customary to attune the painted light to the natural light in the room for which the picture was intended. De Wit did this with great ingenuity. Depending on the distance to the light source, he allowed the contours of the shadows to flow softly or delineated them sharply" (Rijksmuseum story, read end to end). That is the witje's core trick stated as a rule: shadow-edge softness is a function of distance from the (real) light source, so the fake relief's shadows behave like real relief's shadows under the room's actual windows. The 1740 pair also shows the motif-cache at work: seasons as putti plus attributes, repeatable across overdoors, chimneypieces and ceiling corners for decades. FLAG: dimensions and inventory numbers not verified; the Rijksmuseum story does not state that these two exact panels are in the Rijksmuseum collection (they illustrate the story). Do NOT conflate with the Kassel Four Seasons cycle (W20), dated 1751 -- same subject, different works, eleven years apart.

## W20. Allegory of the Four Seasons: Winter (Kassel cycle)

Date 1751 (search-verified, topimpressionists and wahooart: "It dates from 1751"). Medium oil on canvas, grisaille (Web Gallery of Art, read end to end: "This cycle is characteristic of De Wit's works in grisaille... With their graduated greys, these works... imitate stone bas-relief"). Dimensions 216 x 146 cm (search-verified, topimpressionists) -- reported-but-unverified. Current location Staatliche Museen, Kassel, Germany (Schloss Wilhelmshohe, great galleried hall) (Web Gallery of Art, read end to end: "a seasonal cycle commissioned by Landgrave Wilhelm VIII for the great galleried hall at Schloss Wilhelmshohe"; search-verified for the Kassel location and dimensions). What it shows mechanically: the late, export-scale witje: a full four-season cycle for a German landgrave's palace hall, internationally sought-after. The Web Gallery of Art description gives the architectural logic: "The theoretical architectural function of the works is clearly expressed in their organisation as niches with a stele in each. The divinity appropriate to each of the seasons is represented by a bust on a plinth, while a small crowd of putti make merry play with the god's attributes. Winter is represented by Boreas, the North Wind" (Web Gallery of Art, read end to end). So the late witje is a painted niche: bust-on-plinth (fake sculpture) plus putti with attributes (fake life around the sculpture) plus graduated greys (fake stone). The companion Spring is 217 x 145 cm (search-verified) -- the cycle is uniform in size, a set of four near-identical architectural fields. Three years before his death, de Wit is still painting the same motif-cache (seasons plus putti) at palace scale for a foreign prince -- the mechanism had become a product. FLAG: dimensions search-verified only; the Web Gallery of Art page read end to end gives no dimensions or inventory number, and does not date the cycle (1751 comes from secondary art sites).

## Beyond the 20

- **Design for a Ceiling Painting in Three Parts, 1724** -- Rijksmuseum story image 10 (read end to end). The 1724 design drawing; a dated midpoint between the Pels ensemble (1723) and the Surmont pair (1727).
- **Self-Portrait in Medallion, Surrounded by Painter's Attributes** -- Rijksmuseum story image 9, dated "1705-1754" in the story (i.e. undated within his life) (read end to end). A grisaille self-portrait as a fake medallion -- the witje turned on himself.
- **The Epic Poem (Apollo Enthroned with Minerva and the Nine Muses), 1743, Mauritshuis, The Hague** -- oil, 270 x 398 cm (search-verified, wahooart -- reported-but-unverified). The ceiling from a house at Rapenburg 48, Leiden, hanging in the Mauritshuis since 1912 (Smartify "The Comedy", read end to end): Apollo on an oval centrepiece with his nine muses; four grisailles around it -- The Elegy, The Comedy, The Pastoral Song, The Epic Poem -- each now catalogued as a separate Google Arts and Culture asset. The Rijksmuseum story's "Apollo Enthroned in the Clouds, with Minerva and the Nine Muses, 1731" is a different work (or the same iconography twelve years earlier) -- location NOT VERIFIED; do not merge the two.
- **Flora and Zephyr, 1743, Metropolitan Museum of Art** -- oil on canvas, 53 x 63 cm (search-verified) -- a modello for a larger ceiling canvas for Gerrit Hooft's canal house, Amsterdam (search-verified). Not to be confused with the Stadel's Flora and Zephyr, a watercolour and ink drawing of c. 1746 (search-verified, Artera) -- two objects, same subject, different media and dates.
- **A Bacchic allegory with putti at play, signed and dated 1732** -- oil on canvas, Sotheby's 2025 (L25030) (search-verified). A dated mid-career putti piece.
- **Vanitas, 1732, oval 32 x 23 cm (12 1/2 x 9 in), signed "J de Wit" and dated 1732** -- oil on canvas on masonite, Mifflin Smith Collection (acquired Skinner, Boston, 2017; ex G. N. Northrop, c. 1910) (search-verified, SB Conservation). Probably the same picture as Wikipedia's "Allegory of Transience (1733) - Oil on canvas" -- date conflict 1732 vs 1733, FLAG. A third object: Bukowskis' chalk drawing "Allegory of the transience of life", signed and dated 1744, 38.5 x 28.5 cm (search-verified).
- **Hercules and Omphale, signed and dated "JdWit 1721"** -- oil on canvas (old relined), 125 x 139 cm, Hessian private collection (search-verified, Invaluable). The earliest signed-and-dated oil in this leg -- a 1721 anchor between the Antwerp years and the Pels ensemble.
- **Paris and Oenone, 1737, oil on panel, Rijksmuseum** (search-verified, ArtsDot) -- same year as Moses; a small mythological panel against the monumental wall.
- **Putti with Mirrors, 1744, oil, 30 x 35 cm, Rijksmuseum** (search-verified, allpaintingsstore) -- a small late witje; putti with mirror, palette and brushes (the arts as putti-play).
- **Design for a ceiling painting with the Apotheosis of Hercules and four of his labours, 1746** -- wash and ink drawing on paper, Rijksmuseum (Google Arts and Culture asset) (search-verified). "The swirling clouds and figures give a tremendous sense of soaring" (search-verified, Artera) -- a late ceiling design, three years after the Epic Poem.
- **Spring and Summer, 1750, 79 x 132 cm, Laing Art Gallery, Newcastle upon Tyne** (search-verified, wikioo) -- a late easel allegory, three years before his death.
- **Apollo and the Four Seasons, 1750** -- for a house on the Herengracht (search-verified, SB Conservation) -- the same year as Spring and Summer; late ceiling work.
- **Bacchus and Ceres in the Clouds, Huis Bosbeek, Heemstede, 1751** (search-verified, Web Gallery of Art biography) -- late ceiling, same year as the Kassel cycle.
- **Jephthah series (five paintings), 1729-34, 168 Herengracht** -- the best-preserved in-situ de Wit plus de Moucheron ensemble (front room: painted wall hangings by Isaac de Moucheron, ceiling painting and overdoors by de Wit) (Bulletin fig. 10 caption, read end to end; search-verified, SB Conservation). The house now operates as a theatre museum.
- **Allegory of spring and summer and Allegory of the five senses (two designs for the country house "Outshoorn" of Jan Hudde Dedel, mayor of The Hague)** -- pen, ink and wash designs; the only surviving documentation of that interior, the paintings being lost; ex Cornelis Ploos van Amstel collection (search-verified -- leg 2's note above mislabels the source as read end to end; treat as search-verified, see C510).
- **Rubens-copy drawings dated 1750 (Courtauld Institute of Art and Museum Plantin-Moretus)** -- e.g. St Anne and the Virgin, St Mary Magdalene, The Name of Jesus, The Nativity, The Fall of the Rebel Angels, Temptation of Christ (Plantin-Moretus, 1751) (search-verified, Wikimedia Commons "Paintings by Jacob de Wit" category table). De Wit re-drawing the Rubens ceiling panels at age 55, the year before Punt's publication -- the salvage project as a lifelong thread.
- **Ceiling Design, early 18th century, Met 69.296** -- pen and brown ink, brush and brown wash over red chalk, 30.2 x 21.6 cm (search-verified, Met). **Apollo and Thetis, early 18th century, Met 64.197.10**, Rogers Fund 1964 (search-verified, Met).
- **Design for a wall decoration (?) with the arms of Groningen, a faux stucco grisaille and allegorical figures of Truth and Justice** -- red chalk and red chalk wash with grey wash and opaque white, V and A, D.1976-1885 (search-verified). A witje as civic heraldry.
- **Two putti blowing bubbles in a landscape, with vanitas symbols, 1748, Hermitage Museum** (search-verified, Commons category table) -- late vanitas putti.
- **An Allegory of the Arts** -- Rafael Valls, London; ex Richard D. and Billie Lou Wood collection, acquired 8 November 1991 (search-verified). A late allegory (the wahooart snippet dates an "Allegory of the Arts" to 1742, search-verified).
- **Thirteen grisaille paintings of Hebrew Bible subjects, Royal Palace Amsterdam** (part of the Moses scheme) (search-verified, SB Conservation -- single source).
- **Six ceiling paintings for the library of the Moses and Aaron Church, 1729** (search-verified, Onno van Seggelen) -- the church "possessed the largest collection of paintings made by Jacob de Wit in the period 1710-1749" (search-verified).
- **Four Seasons cherub paintings at Hinton Ampner House (Hampshire) and Kingston Lacy House (Dorset)** -- three-dimensional monochrome cherub paintings, in situ in English country houses (Wikipedia "Location of paintings", read end to end).
- **A painting for a door in Heemstede, now in the Linnaeus Museum, Uppsala, Sweden** (Wikipedia, read end to end).
- **Audi alteram partem overdoor, Old City Hall, The Hague** -- see W18.
- **Five angel heads, oil on canvas, 53.5 x 74 cm** -- a ceiling study (varied perspectives suggest a preparatory work), sold Austria 2025 (search-verified, lot-art).
- **Design for a ceiling in the Hudde House, The Hague** (search-verified, meisterdrucke listing).

## Evolution

**1711-1718 -- Antwerp apprenticeship and the copy years.** Red chalk after Rubens (W1), a history painting of Roman virtue (W2), and the first altarpiece (W3, 1716, 260 x 180 cm). The hand is a copyist's and a history painter's: chalk studies of foreshortened bodies seen from below, large devotional canvases for the Catholic network. The Juno study (W4, c. 1716-18) already shows the ceiling idea forming -- a goddess seated on clouds, drawn in the black and red chalk plus wash system -- but learned from prints (Cortona via Bloemaert), not from Rome. Scale is already large (the Baptism altarpiece), but the work is upright and narrative, not architectural.

**1718-1727 -- the canal-house decorator.** Ten ceiling pieces for the Cromhouts (W5, 1718, "strongly influenced by Rubens and Jordaens"), the Rotterdam design (W6, 1725), the Pels Aeneas ensemble (W7-W10, 1723) and the Surmont pair (W13-W14, 1727). The change is structural, not just stylistic: the picture stops being an object and becomes a room system -- ceiling plus overdoors plus chimneypiece plus (with de Moucheron) wall hangings, all light-calibrated to one set of windows, all annotated on the back of the oil sketch with client, year and destination. The oil sketch and modello becomes the working unit (W7, W16). The 1727 dealer note dates the Rococo turn explicitly: "light grace, easy movement and fresh colors in contrast to the former heavy baroque compositions" (Onno, read end to end). Palette lightens; bodies get smaller relative to the cloud field; putti multiply.

**1731-1738 -- monumental and civic scale.** The Callisto ceiling (W15, 1731, whole ceiling 850 x 530 cm), the Moses wall (W17, 1735-37, 5.94 x 13.71 m), The Hague Alderman's Hall (W18, 1738). The private-room system is now applied to public rooms: the same ceiling plus grisaille-corner grammar (W15's brunaille seasons; W18's grisaille virtues) frames a civic argument, and the same light-calibration rule governs a council chamber. The Moses commission also shows the working method at its most deliberate: tapestry-format gouache test, oil sketches in two museums, a municipal-archives study -- the biggest picture of his life, prototyped in three media.

**1740-1751 -- the motif-cache years and the witje as product.** The 1740 Winter and Autumn pair (W19), the 1743 Epic Poem ceiling (see Beyond the 20), the 1746 Hercules ceiling design (Beyond the 20), the 1750 return to Rubens copies (Courtauld sheets, Beyond the 20), the Kassel Four Seasons (W20, 1751). What changes: the invention narrows and the execution standardises. Seasons plus putti plus a god-bust on a plinth, in graduated greys, in uniform sizes (216-217 x 145-146 cm), for a German landgrave -- the same cache he was mining in 1740, now an export commodity. What does not change: the grisaille is still painted as fake architecture (niche plus stele plus plinth), and the light rule still governs. The late return to the Rubens ceiling copies in 1750 closes the circle: the 55-year-old copies the same panels the 16-year-old copied, a year before Punt publishes them.

**Palette and handling, in one line:** heavy Flemish Baroque colour and dense bodies (1716-1723), then light Rococo grace, airy cloud fields, fresh colour (1727 onward), then monochrome discipline as a parallel track throughout (grisaille and brunaille from the 1723 Pels corners to the 1751 Kassel cycle). The grisaille never "develops" into colour; it is the constant, and the colour ceilings get lighter around it. Scale grows monotonically: 340 mm chalk study, then 51.5 cm oil sketch, then 240 cm wall pair, then 566 cm ceiling, then 13.7 m civic wall.

## Disputes and unverified items (flagged)

- **"(1685-1754)" header.** The Susan Brown Conservation page titles de Wit "(1685-1754)" and repeats "(1685 - 1754)" in its vanitas section, while its own body text gives "baptized December 19, 1695". Every other source read (Wikipedia, Rijksmuseum, Jeffares, Onno, Web Gallery of Art, ULAN) gives 1695. 1685 is a typo; 19 December 1695 (baptism) is correct. The #540 log already flagged this header; this study confirms it from the page read end to end.
- **Married or never married?** Onno van Seggelen: "He married Cornelia Leonora van Neck in 1720 and the couple remained childless." Rafael Valls corroborates a wife ("Both he and his wife bought houses on the Keizersgracht"). NiceArtGallery states flatly: "He never married". Direct contradiction; unresolved on the evidence read. The Onno account cites Staring's 1958 monograph and is preferred, but the study must not present the marriage as settled.
- **Move to Antwerp: 1708 vs 1710.** Onno (Allegory page): "About 1708 Jacob went to live for several years with his uncle... in Antwerp." Rafael Valls: "In 1710 de Wit moved to Antwerp." Onno (Jupiter page): "His parents decided in 1710 to move to Antwerp." Two dates from the same dealer's pages; unresolved.
- **Van Hal apprenticeship vs Academy dates.** Onno (Jupiter page): apprenticed to Jacob van Hal "From 1709 to 1711", then "from 1711 onwards he drew from live models at the Royal Antwerp Academy". SB Conservation: Academy "from 1711 to 1713". If the move was 1710, a 1709 apprenticeship is impossible; if the move was 1708, the dates fit. The 1709-1711 apprenticeship is second-hand (dealer page) and conflicts with the 1710 move date.
- **Guild of St Luke: 1713 vs 1714.** SB Conservation: "In 1713 de Wit joined the Antwerp Guild of St. Luke." Wikipedia (citing RKD): member in 1714. Onno (Jupiter page): "In 1713-1714, he was registered as a master at the Guild of Saint Luke." All three versions exist; the RKD page itself was NOT read end to end, so the RKD's own wording is second-hand here.
- **36 drawings vs 39 ceiling paintings.** The British Museum group is 36 drawings after Rubens's ceiling panels (Rooses 1-36); Onno: "He made thirty-six drawings". The Carolus Borromeuskerk article and the TOPA project page say Rubens made 39 ceiling pieces. The gap (36 vs 39) is unexplained in sources read -- whether three panels were never copied, lost, or counted differently is NOT VERIFIED. Rafael Valls adds a third number: "the decorations he executed for the thirty-six chapels in the Jesuit church" -- which confuses drawings with chapels and is probably an error.
- **Return to Amsterdam: 1715 vs 1716 vs 1715-17.** SB Conservation and Wikipedia: "About 1715". Onno (Jupiter page): "Around 1715-1716". Jeffares: "on his return to Amsterdam in 1716". Onno (Allegory page): "returned to Holland between 1715 and 1717". A one-to-two-year window, not a date.
- **Rubens-copy dates: 1711-1722 vs 1711-1712.** Onno: copied "during 1711-1722 in red chalks". NiceArtGallery: "around 1711-1712". The 1711-1722 range (eleven years for 36 small copies) is suspiciously long and is second-hand; the narrower date is from a low-quality source. Unresolved. The span also straddles the 18 July 1718 fire; copies dated after 1718 must derive from de Wit's own earlier drawings, not from the ceilings themselves.
- **Moses commission: dates, room, size.** (a) Chosen 1735 (Agnes Etherington) vs "most prestigious commission" 1736 (Rafael Valls) vs painted Nov 1735 - Oct 1737 (Google Arts and Culture) vs completed December 1737 (Wikipedia). (b) Room: Council Chamber and Vroedschapskamer (Google Arts and Culture; Wikipedia) vs Vierschaar, the tribunal room (NiceArtGallery). The Vroedschapskamer version is corroborated by the 36-councillors reading and is preferred; Vierschaar is likely an error. (c) Size: 5.940 x 13.710 m (Wikipedia) vs "over 5 by 12.5 metres" (Rijksmuseum story). The Wikipedia figure is the more precise; the Rijksmuseum figure may be rounded or may describe a different measurement.
- **Pupils: six named vs "no real pupils".** Wikipedia (citing RKD): Jan de Groot, Dionys van Nijmegen, Jan Punt, Pieter Tanje, Frans and Jacob Xavery. Onno: "De Wit had no real pupils and the only two artists mentioned as having been taught some basics by him are Johannes de Groot and Gerrit Dadelbeek." Jeffares: Jacob Xavery "lived with him". The RKD list is second-hand (RKD page not read); the Onno claim is also second-hand (dealer page citing Staring). Only Jan Punt is firmly attested in both traditions (the 1751 engravings). Further: Getty ULAN lists de Wit as "student of Jan de Groot (1650-1726)" -- conflicts with the RKD teacher list (van Spiers, van Hal) and is most likely a database conflation of the two de Groots (the Hague pupil Johannes de Groot vs Jan de Groot, 1650-1726, pupil of Adriaen van Ostade, already carried as a #535 extra spare).
- **Estate-sale figures unverified.** NiceArtGallery: inventory of "between 2,500 and 3,000 drawings and paintings", sale 1755, proceeds "3,300 Dutch guilders". The Bulletin (scholarly, read end to end) says only that "there were more than eighty oil sketches for ceiling paintings" at the 1755 sale. The 2,500-3,000 and 3,300-guilder figures come from a single low-quality gallery page with no citation; NOT VERIFIED. Do not use in the study without a better source.
- **"Original set of copies destroyed by fire in 1718".** SB Conservation: "His original set of copies was destroyed by fire in 1718, though de Wit made a second, more-finished set". It is not explained how de Wit's own copies -- presumably in his possession in Antwerp or Amsterdam -- were destroyed by the same church fire that destroyed the Rubens originals, nor which fire is meant. The claim is second-hand and unexplained; the Bulletin and Wikipedia say only that the originals burned and the copies became the historical record.
- **1696 birth variant.** Getty ULAN lists one source giving "(Dutch artist, 1696-1754)" [WL-Courtauld] against the consensus 1695. Second-hand, single-source; almost certainly a rounding and baptism-year error, but noted. The Boijmans artist line "Amsterdam 1695/1696" is the same old-style year-boundary wobble.
