- **Shoemaker father and watchmaker mother.** NiceArtGallery only: "his father was a shoemaker, and his mother came from a family of watchmakers." No corroboration in any source read end to end. Second-hand only; do not present as fact without a better source. (Onno's "lower middle class" is the verified version.)
- **Design+Encyclopedia is unreliable.** A search result from design-encyclopedia.com claims de Wit was "renowned for his ceiling paintings of churches and theatres, his frescoes, and his tapestries. His set designs for operas, such as Handel's Giustino, were highly praised". No other source read mentions opera sets, frescoes or tapestries; the page is generic AI-style filler. NOT USED anywhere in this study.
- **RKD page NOT read end to end.** No direct RKD artist-page URL was returned by any search, so the RKD record (artist no. 85099, confirmed via ULAN and Wikimedia Commons authority data) was not read. Every "according to the RKD" claim in this study (teachers, pupils, follower, Guild date) is second-hand via EN Wikipedia and must be labelled as such.
- **"Specially equipment" truncated.** The Rijksmuseum story says "De Wit painted these ceilings using a specially equipment." The sentence is garbled and truncated in the page text as served; what equipment (scaffolding? a painting frame? a mirror system?) is NOT described in any source read. Do not invent a ceiling-painting apparatus.
- **"Thirty-six chapels".** Rafael Valls: "Some of his major works were the decorations he executed for the thirty-six chapels in the Jesuit church in Antwerp." The Jesuit church did not have thirty-six chapels decorated by de Wit; the number 36 belongs to his Rubens-copy drawings. Almost certainly a dealer-page error; not used.
- **The Hague ceiling date vs Moses.** SB Conservation dates the Old Town Hall of The Hague Alderman's Hall ceiling to 1738; the Moses Town Hall scheme ran Nov 1735-Oct 1737. The two civic commissions therefore overlap and sequence tightly (1737 Amsterdam, 1738 The Hague) -- plausible, but the 1738 date is single-sourced (SB Conservation) and NOT VERIFIED against the Hague's own records.
- **"First Dutch artist since the 16th century" (Catholic decorative work).** Web Gallery of Art: "He was a Catholic and was the first Dutch artist since the 16th century to carry out a good deal of decorative work for Catholic churches." A sweeping claim, single-sourced (Web Gallery of Art biography), not verified against any survey. Treat as the Web Gallery of Art's judgement, not fact.
- **Baptism of Christ medium conflict.** Oil on canvas (art sites) vs chalk and pen drawing (Wikipedia major works). Unresolved; most likely two objects (a study and the altarpiece).
- **Vanitas and Allegory of Transience: 1732 vs 1733.** The signed Vanitas is dated 1732 (SB Conservation); Wikipedia lists "Allegory of Transience (1733)". A separate chalk drawing of the same theme is signed and dated 1744. Probably the same picture under two dates; a third object exists.
- **Winter: 1740 vs 1751.** The Rijksmuseum story's 1740 Winter and Autumn pair vs the Kassel Winter of 1751 -- different works, same subject; do not conflate.
- **Flora and Zephyr: 1743 vs c. 1746.** The 1743 Met oil modello vs the c. 1746 Stadel watercolour drawing -- two objects, same subject.
- **Apollo Enthroned (1731, Rijksmuseum story) vs The Epic Poem (1743, Mauritshuis).** Similar iconography, different dates; the 1731 work's location is NOT VERIFIED.
- **W5 vs W4: possible double-counting.** The "ten ceiling pieces 1718" for Cromhout may be the same commission as the 1717-18 Herengracht 366 decoration -- see C513.
- **Alupka and St Petersburg identifications (W8-W10).** The Bulletin authors' attribution; the holding museums list them as anonymous 18th-century works.
- **Herengracht 366 Juno connection (W4).** Expert opinion (van den Hout), not documentary.
- **Mucius Scaevola (W2).** Attribution rests on a dealer page alone; no medium or dimensions.
- **British Museum object pages.** Returned only boilerplate when opened (46 lines, no object data); all BM dimensions in this study are search-verified from snippets of those same records, not read end to end.
- **"Thirteen grisaille Hebrew Bible paintings" in the Royal Palace.** Single secondary source (SB Conservation).
- **The Outshoorn designs.** Search-verified only, not read end to end -- leg 2's note above mislabels the source; treat as search-verified (see C510).

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

Under the definition established in #533 (museum or auction page read end to end): VERIFIED for 8 of the 20 works -- W4 Juno study 340 x 241 mm (Sotheby's); W6 Zephyr and Flora design 261 x 200 mm (Boijmans); W7 Apotheosis of Aeneas oil sketch 51.5 x 69.5 cm (Bulletin); W8 Juno Asks Aeolus 116.5 x 80 cm (Bulletin); W9 Venus Asks Jupiter 116 x 96 cm (Bulletin); W10 Putti around an Altar 84 x 181 cm (Bulletin); W13 Jupiter and Callisto 240 x 205 cm (Onno, citing SK-A-3885); W15 Assumption of Callisto ceiling, centre 360 x 535 cm, whole ceiling 850 x 530 cm (Bulletin). Reported-but-unverified for 7 -- W1 Rubens-copy sheets c. 335 x 394-398 mm (BM records via search snippets); W3 Baptism of Christ 260 x 180 cm (art sites); W11 Holy Family and Trinity 140 x 105 cm (ArtsDot); W12 Adoration of the Shepherds 138 x 103 cm (topimpressionists); W16 Diana modello 49.5 x 62.8 cm (Fondation Custodia); W17 Moses 5.94 x 13.71 m (Wikipedia and Commons, against the Rijksmuseum story's "over 5 by 12.5 m"); W20 Kassel Winter 216 x 146 cm (topimpressionists). NOT VERIFIED for 5 -- W2 Mucius Scaevola (no medium, no dimensions); W5 ten Cromhout ceiling pieces (no dimensions); W14 Jupiter and Mnemosyne (no dimensions); W18 The Hague Alderman's Hall ceiling (no dimensions); W19 Winter and Autumn pair (no dimensions or inventory numbers). 8 + 7 + 5 = 20.

## Aesthetic logic

De Wit's aesthetic is the logic of the room, not the logic of the picture. Everything in his system serves one question: does this compartment belong to the architecture, or does it hang on it? The witje answers by pretending to be stone, and then by matching its grey to the panelling so the pretence is not a joke but a fit. The coloured ceiling answers the same question from the other side: it opens the ceiling into sky, so the room gains a heaven instead of a lid. Between them, the light rule is the conscience of the whole system -- painted light must agree with real light, because the moment it disagrees the illusion is not just broken, it is provably foreign, as the Callisto ceiling proves. His palette logic follows: colour for the heavens in the middle, monochrome for the architecture at the edges -- the virtues at The Hague, the seasons in the Pels corners, the gods on plinths at Kassel -- so the allegory reads as structure and the gods float free of it. The putti are the connective tissue: small, repeatable, combinable bodies that can be set in any compartment in either register, colour or stone, and the repetition is not laziness but the point -- a typeface needs few letters to say many things. The programme logic is social: the subject is chosen for the patron, Aeneas for the self-made banker, Diana for the widow, Moses for the council, so the room flatters its owner in a language the guests already know. And the archive logic is the afterlife: the oil sketch annotated on the back, the inscription naming the client and the room, the scale marks that let a ceiling be rebuilt from a 51.5 cm sketch -- de Wit's system is designed to survive its rooms, and it did, because the paper trail outlived the panelling. What holds it all together is thrift turned into a signature: grisaille imitates sculpture because sculpture is more expensive than paint, and de Wit turned that economy into a genre named after himself. The witje is honest about its own trick -- white paint pretending to be white stone -- and the joke (Wit means white) is part of the brand.

## Positions

**P1 -- De Wit painted architecture, not pictures.** His unit of work was the room: ceiling, overdoors, chimney-pieces and wall panels as one fixed ensemble (M5). A de Wit "painting" removed from its compartment is a fragment of a larger machine, which is why so many survive as orphans (the Aeneas sketch, the Russian overdoors) and why the study must reconstruct rooms, not just list works.

**P2 -- The witje is thrift made into a signature.** Grisaille imitates sculpture because sculpture is more expensive than paint (M1); de Wit turned that economy into a genre named after himself. The mechanism is honest about its own trick -- white paint pretending to be white stone -- and the joke (Wit = white) is part of the brand.

**P3 -- Light is the load-bearing element.** Matching painted light to real light (M2) is not a finishing touch; it is what makes the illusion hold, and its failure is what exposes a swapped ceiling. De Wit's most reliable "signature" is not his handwriting but the direction of his shadows.

**P4 -- The ceiling is a sky you enter, not a picture you look at.** Di sotto in su (M8) means the composition only works from below, from a specific standing point in a specific room. The work is completed by the viewer's body position -- a spatial, not pictorial, contract.

**P5 -- Copying is preservation, not derivativeness.** The Rubens copies (M6) began as a student's exercise and ended as the only surviving record of a destroyed masterpiece. De Wit's career is bookended by copying: he learned by copying Rubens, and his own work survives largely because others copied, engraved and annotated it.

**P6 -- The putti are a typeface.** The motif cache (M7) works like movable type: small bodies plus attributes, set in any compartment, in colour or monochrome, at any scale. The art is in the setting -- the recombination -- not in inventing a new letter each time.

**P7 -- Catholic in a Protestant city, and paid by both.** De Wit's career crosses the confessional divide (schuilkerk altarpieces, then Town Hall Moses), which suggests his market was competence and fashion, not confession. The Moses commission -- a Catholic painting a Hebrew Bible scene for the Protestant city council -- is the proof: the council bought the allegory, not the artist's religion.

**P8 -- The room is a social machine.** The painted room existed for games, conversation and display (M5); the ceiling gave the merchant a glimpse of the heaven his endeavours had earned (M11). Decoration is status technology: it tells guests who the owner is and what he deserves.

**P9 -- The archive is the work's second body.** Inscriptions, oil sketches, sale catalogues and deeds (M10, M4) are what allow a lost room to be rebuilt on paper two centuries later. De Wit's habit of annotating his sketches is as much a part of his system as his brushwork -- without it, the Aeneas and Callisto mix-up would be unsolvable.

**P10 -- Loss is part of the oeuvre.** Fire (1718), whitewash (the corner seasons, hidden under plaster), sale to Russia, remodelling -- de Wit's work has been destroyed, covered, sold off and swapped more than most. The study's subject is therefore not only what he painted but what happened to it: the hybrid ceiling at 479 Herengracht is as characteristic of his afterlife as any intact room.

## Quotes

All quotes are verbatim from pages read end to end. No words BY Jacob de Wit survive in any source read, except short Dutch inscriptions on his own drawings (see Q12 and M10). Every quote below is ABOUT him, by a museum, scholar or contemporary commentator.

- **Q1** (Rijksmuseum, Dutch Masters story): "De Wit was a master of grisaille, a technique of monochrome painting in shades of white and grey to create the illusion of a marble sculpture in low relief."
- **Q2** (Rijksmuseum): "It was customary to attune the painted light to the natural light in the room for which the picture was intended. De Wit did this with great ingenuity."
- **Q3** (Rijksmuseum): "De Wit's interior paintings were not independent pictures, but had a fixed place in the interior."
- **Q4** (Rijksmuseum): "Together with the portraitist Jan Maurits Quinkhard, he was Amsterdam's highest paid painter. In 1742, his annual income was valued at 4000 guilders and he lived in style on Keizersgracht."
- **Q5** (Web Gallery of Art biography): "De Wit's style, however, was much lighter than Rubens's, with a Rococo delicacy and charm."
- **Q6** (Web Gallery of Art biography): "His name has entered the Dutch language to describe a kind of trompe-l'oeil imitation of marble reliefs for which he was renowned; such pictures, usually set over a chimney-piece or door, are called 'witjes ('wit' is Dutch for 'white')."
- **Q7** (Rijksmuseum Bulletin, De Fouw and Verslype): "As was usual at that time, De Wit coordinated the painted incidence of the light in his interior paintings with the direction of the natural light in a room."
- **Q8** (Rijksmuseum Bulletin): "De Wit made oil sketches as an interim step between the initial design and the execution of the work to give the client an idea of the eventual result."
- **Q9** (Rijksmuseum Bulletin): "Pinholes indicate that he used a compass to draw the curves around the central field."
- **Q10** (Rijksmuseum Bulletin): "It is virtually inconceivable that De Wit could have lit the principal composition incorrectly."
- **Q11** (EN Wikipedia, "Jacob de Wit"): "After the church was struck by lightning in 1718 these became a historical document, and his pupil Jan Punt later engraved his sketches and published them in 1751."
- **Q12** (Onno van Seggelen, "Jupiter and Callisto", quoting the inscription on the back of de Wit's preparatory drawing -- the closest thing to his own words found): "Vor de Heer Jonas Witsen / schepen / met Corikeuren getekent" ["Drawn for Mr Jonas Witsen, alderman, with ..."]. Note: the Bulletin also records his annotation "voor d'Heer pieterpels in sijne Edl sijkaemer 1723" on the Pels design drawing. Both are working inscriptions, not statements of artistic intent.

## Network

Teachers: 1. Albert van Spiers -- Amsterdam painter and decorator of canal houses; de Wit apprenticed to him from age 9 to 13 (Rafael Valls; Onno van Seggelen); van Spiers had himself been a pupil of Gerard de Lairesse. RKD via the Wikipedia de Wit article. FLAG: no dates for van Spiers were found in two searches; no dedicated biography page exists. Proposed as a spare (see Future candidates) with no dates. 2. Jacob van Hal (1672-1750) -- Antwerp history painter; de Wit apprenticed to him 1709-1711 (Onno van Seggelen) after moving to Antwerp to live with his uncle; RKD via the Wikipedia de Wit article. Dates search-verified only: Getty ULAN "Hal, Jacob van (Flemish painter, 1672-1750)" and Rafael Valls "Jakob van Hal (1672-1750)". Spelling variants Jacob and Jakob van Hal. 3. Gerard de Lairesse (b. Liege 1640 - d. Amsterdam 1711) -- teacher of van Spiers, hence de Wit's teacher's teacher; his Groot Schilderboek (1707) informed de Wit's classicizing composition (NiceArtGallery); Onno van Seggelen lists Lairesse among de Wit's strongest influences, with Rubens and van Dyck. Already carried as a #529 spare -- NOT a new candidate. 4. Uncle Jacomo -- de Wit's wealthy Antwerp uncle and godfather; de Wit lived with him from c.1708/1710 and met van Hal in his circle; Jacomo refused to fund a proposed Rome journey (Rafael Valls; Onno van Seggelen). Family host, not a maker.

Pupils (RKD list, via the Wikipedia de Wit article, read end to end): 5. Jan de Groot, called Johannes de Groot -- painter from The Hague; named in the RKD pupil list on de Wit's article. CONFLICT: Onno van Seggelen says "De Wit had no real pupils and the only two artists mentioned as having been taught some basics by him are Johannes de Groot and Gerrit Dadelbeek", while Getty ULAN's "teacher of" list names Dadelbeek, Johannes de Groot, Jan Punt and Jan Stolker. NAME TRAP: Jan de Groot (1650-1726), pupil of Adriaen van Ostade, is a different man and is already carried as a #535 extra spare. The Hague pupil's dates were not found in this leg. 6. Dionys van Nijmegen (1705-1798) -- Rotterdam; pupil of his father Elias van Nijmegen and of de Wit; joined the Rotterdam Guild of St Luke in 1743; portraits and wall and ceiling decorations; father and teacher of Gerard van Nijmegen. (Wikipedia read end to end, 22 lines.) 7. Jan Punt (1711-1779) -- pupil; engraved de Wit's copies of Rubens's ceiling paintings, published Amsterdam 1751 (De Plafonds...); the Met holds Coronation of the Virgin, 1751 (51.501.7267) and Temptation of Christ, 1747 (51.501.7255), both "Drawn by Jacob de Wit, Engraved by Jan Punt"; also engraved the Teekenboek der Proportien after de Wit, 1747 (RISD). 8. Pieter Tanje (1706-1761) -- pupil at the Amsterdam Stadsacademie from age 24, under Bernard Picart, Jacob Houbraken, Cornelis Troost and Jacob de Wit (RKD via Wikipedia, read end to end, 18 lines); engraved more than 100 plates for Jan van Gool's Nieuwe Schouburg (1750), mostly from portrait sketches by Aert Schouman. 9. Frans Xavery (bapt. 15 January 1740 - c.1788) -- studied first under his uncle Gerard Joseph Xavery, afterwards under de Wit; joined the Pictura Society, The Hague, 1768; last dated work a 1788 series of five landscapes with figures for the castle at Turnhout. (Wikipedia read end to end, 16 lines.) 10. Jacob Xavery (bapt. 27 April 1736 - after 1771) -- pupil of de Wit; feigned bas-reliefs in his master's manner; also trained by J. van Huysem (Jan van Huysum) per the pastellists; practised in Amsterdam, Breda, The Hague and Paris; portraits including Gerrit Braamcamp. (Wikipedia read end to end, 22 lines.) FLAG: death "after 1771" (Wikipedia) vs pastellists "p.1779". 11. Jacobus Buys (19 November 1724 - 7 April 1801) -- studied under Cornelis Pronk, Jacob de Wit and Cornelis Troost; director of the Amsterdam Drawing Academy; joined the Guild of St Luke in 1750; bas-reliefs, tapestry designs, book illustrations and theatre scenes. (Wikipedia read end to end, 20 lines.) FLAG: Buys is NOT in the RKD pupil list on de Wit's article; the link rests on Buys's own article (Bryan's Dictionary); the British Museum biography names only Pronk and Troost as his teachers and omits de Wit; Sotheby's calls him a pupil of Troost only. 12. Gerrit Dadelbeek -- taught "some basics" by de Wit (Onno van Seggelen; ULAN teacher-of list). No dates found in this leg; thin record -- name only, not proposed. 13. Jan Stolker -- Getty ULAN lists him as a pupil of de Wit. No dates found in this leg; thin record -- name only, not proposed.

Follower: 14. Tako Hajo Jelgersma (29 October 1702 - 24 March 1795) -- follower (RKD via Wikipedia); his grisailles followed de Wit's manner; pupil of Frans Decker and Wigerus Vitringa; moved to Haarlem in 1752; pupils Johan Bernard Brandhoff, Johannes Petrus van Horstok, Cornelis van Noorde, Martinus van der Jagt and Johannes Swertner; lived to 92. (Wikipedia read end to end, 23 lines.)

Collaborator: 15. Isaac de Moucheron (1667-1744) -- painted the wall hangings while de Wit painted the ceiling (Apotheosis of Aeneas, with Four Seasons in the corners) in the room of Pieter Pels, Herengracht 479; Rijksmuseum Bulletin article by Josephina de Fouw and Ige Verslype. Already carried as a #538 spare -- NOT a new candidate.

The Rubens line: 16. Peter Paul Rubens (1577-1640) -- de Wit copied Rubens's 39 ceiling paintings for the Antwerp Jesuit church (Carolus Borromeuskerk) in watercolour and red chalk, 1711-1722 (Onno van Seggelen; Wikipedia); the church was struck by lightning on 18 July 1718 and all 39 paintings were destroyed (topa.be; Wikipedia, St Charles Borromeo); de Wit's copies became the historical record and were engraved by Jan Punt, published 1751. Rubens's designs were executed mostly by his pupils, including Anthony van Dyck (Wikipedia). FLAG: the 1711-1722 copy span straddles the 1718 fire; copies dated after 1718 must derive from de Wit's own earlier drawings, not from the ceilings themselves. 17. Anthony van Dyck (1599-1641) -- executed Rubens's ceiling designs; de Wit studied van Dyck's works in Antwerp (Rafael Valls; Onno van Seggelen). Already carried as a #524 candidate -- NOT a new candidate.

Patrons and commissioners: 18. Amsterdam Town Hall (now Royal Palace, Dam Square) -- 1736 commission for the Council Chamber; Moses Choosing the Seventy Elders (1737), over 5 by 12.5 m, plus some 13 grisaille paintings of Hebrew Bible subjects (Rijksmuseum; SB Conservation). FLAG: commission dated 1736 (Rafael Valls) against painting dated 1737. 19. Pieter Pels (1668-1739) -- Amsterdam merchant; Apotheosis of Aeneas ceiling for Herengracht 479, with Moucheron wall hangings; oil sketch in the Rijksmuseum (SK-A-4659). (Rijksmuseum Bulletin.) 20. Jan Hudde Dedel (1702-1777), mayor of The Hague -- country house Outshoorn; designs for an Allegory of spring and summer and an Allegory of the five senses (Onno van Seggelen). 21. Jan Baptist de Surmont, Lord of Vlooswijk -- Catholic merchant and banker; 1727 wall decoration Jupiter and Callisto and Jupiter and Mnemosyne for his country seat at Loenen aan de Vecht (now Rijksmuseum) (Onno van Seggelen). 22. Jonas Witsen (1705-1767) -- commissioned the Jupiter and Callisto drawing six years after the painting (Onno van Seggelen). 23. Cornelis Wittert van Valkenburgh -- ceiling design Zephyr and Flora with Companions and Putti in the Clouds for his house on the Delftse Vaart, Rotterdam, 1725 (Boijmans). 24. Catharina Adriana Meerman de la Court -- patron of painted ceilings with putti (pastellists). 25. Father Aegidius de Glabbais -- priest of the Moses and Aaron schuilkerk (hidden Catholic church); altarpieces from 1716, portrait 1718; Baptism of Christ altarpiece for Het Hart (now Museum Ons' Lieve Heer op Solder). 26. The Hague Old Town Hall -- 1738 ceiling for the Alderman's Hall, framed by grisaille putti (Freedom, Industry, Temperance, Fortitude) in the four corners (SB Conservation). 27. Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (1726-1798) -- leading collector and connoisseur; owned many de Wit drawings, including the Outshoorn designs (Onno van Seggelen); also painted by Buys in 1766. Collector, not a commissioner.

Contemporaries and adjacent makers: 28. Cornelis Troost (8 October 1696 - 7 March 1750) -- Amsterdam's other leading 18th-century painter; teacher of Buys and Tanje; pupil of Arnold Boonen; actor turned painter; the NELRI series (Mauritshuis, 1739-40). Proposed as a new main 4. 29. Jan Maurits Quinkhard (28 January 1688 - 11 November 1772) -- with de Wit, Amsterdam's highest-paid painter; 1742 income 4,000 guilders (Rijksmuseum); Panpoeticon Batavum miniatures; founding member of the Amsterdam Stadstekenacademie. Proposed as a spare. 30. Jan van Gool (1685-1763) -- author of the Nieuwe Schouburg (1750), illustrated by Tanje's 100+ plates after Schouman's sketches. Already carried as a #536 spare -- NOT a new candidate. 31. Aert Schouman (4 March 1710 - 5 July 1792) -- his portrait sketches were the models for Tanje's van Gool plates; wall-hangings and chimneypieces; head of the Dordrecht Guild 1742-1792; bird cabinet for William V, 1786. Proposed as a spare. 32. Jan van Huysum (15 April 1682 - 8 February 1749) -- trained Jacob Xavery (pastellists: "trained by Jacob de Wit and J. van Huysem"); the age's best flower painter. Proposed as a spare. 33. Arnold Boonen (1669-1729) -- teacher of Troost and of Quinkhard. Network only. 34. Nicolaas Verkolje (1673-1746) -- teacher of Quinkhard; son of Johannes Verkolje. Network only. 35. Arnold van Halen (1673-1732) -- originator of the Panpoeticon Batavum; Quinkhard painted 100+ of its miniature poet portraits. Network only. 36. Elias van Nijmegen -- father and first teacher of Dionys van Nijmegen. Network only; dates not found in this leg. 37. Gerard van Nijmegen -- son and pupil of Dionys van Nijmegen. Network only; dates not found in this leg. 38. Jan Baptist Xavery -- sculptor; father of Frans and Jacob Xavery. Network only; dates not verified in this leg. 39. Gerard Joseph Xavery -- uncle and first teacher of Frans Xavery. Network only; dates not found in this leg. 40. Cornelis van Noorde -- pupil of Jelgersma; engraved many portraits after Jelgersma, often the only surviving likenesses of the sitters. Network only; dates not found in this leg. 41. Frans Decker and Wigerus Vitringa -- teachers of Jelgersma. Network only; dates not found in this leg. 42. Bernard Picart and Jacob Houbraken -- Tanje's fellow teachers at the Amsterdam Stadsacademie (Houbraken already carried as a #535 spare). Network only. 43. Cornelis Pronk -- teacher of Buys (with de Wit and Troost). Network only; dates not verified in this leg. 44. Gerrit Braamcamp -- Amsterdam patron and collector; portrait subject of Jacob Xavery; his collection sale was 1771 (pastellists). Patron and collector, network only.

Influence line (absence as a finding): no named pupil of de Wit is found carrying the ceiling-painting trade forward as a school: no academy post, no successor named, and the Onno van Seggelen study says flatly "De Wit had no real pupils" -- in direct conflict with the RKD pupil list above. The documented influence line is narrow: Jelgersma's grisailles in de Wit's manner (entry 14) and Jacob Xavery's feigned bas-reliefs in his master's manner (entry 10). NiceArtGallery's summary matches: "While he did not have a large school of direct followers, his style influenced decorative painting in the Netherlands throughout the 18th century" -- but no further named artists are attached to that influence in the sources read this leg.

## Future candidates
Top pick for #542 is Jan Punt (1711-1779; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who put his master's Rubens copies into print, and so saved the ceilings a second time. Engraver; de Wit's pupil; engraved de Wit's copies of Rubens's ceiling paintings, published Amsterdam 1751 (De Plafonds of Gallery-stukken uit de Kerk der P.P. Jesuiten, te Antwerpen, geschilderd door P.P. Rubens, geteekend door J. de Wit, en op koper gebragt door Jan Punt), and the Teekenboek der Proportien after de Wit, 1747; the Met holds Coronation of the Virgin, 1751 (51.501.7267) and Temptation of Christ, 1747 (51.501.7255), both "Drawn by Jacob de Wit, Engraved by Jan Punt". He is the top pick because he is the only firmly attested pupil in both traditions (the RKD list and the "no real pupils" account agree on him, by the 1751 engravings), and because his mechanism is the print as a second salvage: the engraving that carries a drawing that carries a painting that no longer exists. A study of Punt carries the chain from the man who copied Rubens to the man who copied the copy, and from the ceiling to the book. The doorway is the pupil who put his master's Rubens copies into print -- and so saved the ceilings a second time. Painter; pupil of de Wit; feigned bas-reliefs in his master's manner; also trained by Jan van Huysum; worked in Amsterdam, Breda, The Hague and Paris; portraits including Gerrit Braamcamp. The doorway is the pupil who painted fake stone exactly like his master -- and flowers like van Huysum. (The 14 names carried in #540 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem), Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left. German-born Dutch painter, draughtsman, calligrapher and printmaker, the leading Dutch marine painter after both Willem van de Veldes left for England in 1672. He came to Amsterdam around 1650 as a merchant's clerk, taught himself to draw ships, studied under Allaert van Everdingen and Hendrick Dubbels, joined the painters' guild in 1663, and became famous for violent water - he went out to sea in an open boat to study storms. He painted the great View of Amsterdam and the IJ (1665) as a present for Louis XIV's minister, opened a gallery on the top floor of the Amsterdam town hall in 1699, and was visited by Cosimo III de' Medici and Peter the Great. His pupils and followers include Jan Claesz Rietschoof, Aernout Smit, Michiel Maddersteg and Abraham Storck. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the hub of the whole marine spare set - three of this study's spares are his pupils or followers - so studying him unlocks all of them, and his open-boat storm study is a mechanism no other candidate offers. The doorway is the clerk-turned-calligrapher who rowed out into storms in an open boat to learn how water breaks, and then taught half of Amsterdam's marine painters. Burial: Wikipedia gives 12 November 1708 at Westerkerk; BADA gives buried 17 November 1708. Death 7 November 1708 is consistent across sources. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. These look like two Molenaers conflated under one name; do not mix them. Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Marine painter; pupil of Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff; painted in an attic room on the Singel, probably employed by dealer Laurens Cornelisz de Coninck; follower and copyist of Ludolf Bakhuizen (1687/1689 notarial records of copying Bakhuizen); The Africa in Table Bay; most marines 1667-1678, later style tending to Bakhuizen; Amsterdam Guild of St Luke 1688; died aged about 70, buried at the Leiden cemetery. The doorway is the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who built his patron real ships. Marine painter; studied under Ludolf Bakhuizen; spent most of his life at the court of Frederick I of Prussia, returned to Amsterdam later in life, where he commissioned the building of two beautiful ships for Frederick I's pleasure; most of his paintings are in Germany. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. The doorway is the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. FLAG: the Wikipedia lead says 9 January 1689, the body says 1 January 1689 - an internal conflict in the same article. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Daniel de Koninck (1668, Amsterdam - 1730, England; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S27). Nephew of Philips Koninck; apprenticed to his uncle Jacob Koninck at the Copenhagen court 1687-1690; moved to England 1690 (Oxford, then London by 1720); genre works and portraits/tronies in the manner of Rembrandt; Peter King, 1st Baron King of Ockham (1720, National Portrait Gallery). Held back because he is a portraitist, off the landscape line. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. FLAG: death 1730 (Wikipedia) vs c.1720 (Christie's, last known work 1720). Hendrik Kobell (13 September 1751, Rotterdam - 3 August 1779, Rotterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S29). Dutch landscape and marine painter, etcher, draftsman, watercolourist; cousin of Ferdinand and Franz Kobell; father trained him in drawing, he loved drawing ships in Rotterdam port; London 1769-1770, Paris 1772, Amsterdam Stadstekenakademie; founder of the Rotterdam drawing society Hierdoor tot Hooger; father and teacher of Jan Kobell II (1778-1814); pupils Carel Frederik Bendorp and Gerrit van der Pals; The Shipwreck (1775, Rijksmuseum SK-A-1218). Held back because he died at 27 and the record is short. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. FLAG: death 3 August 1779 (Wikipedia, Stadel) vs 11 August 1779 (NiceArtGallery) vs 1782 (wahooart, and Commons gives "3 August 1779 / 1782"); wahooart's "died of mental illness" is uncorroborated; London trip 1769 (Wikipedia) vs 1770 (WGA). Dominique Michael Serres (also Dominic Michael Serres; c. 1763 - 1816; dates search-verified only, from the Government Art Collection). Younger son of Dominic Serres; landscape painter (the only Serres who left the sea); served in the Royal Navy on HMS Foudroyant 1782; married Lucretia Madden 4 August 1788; prisoner of the King's Bench Prison by December 1813; died 1816; View of the Teign, Devon (GAC). Held back because his surviving work is small and mostly watercolour landscape. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. FLAG: dates search-verified only; Crouch Rare Books gives c.1761-after 1804 against GAC c.1763-1816; artsdot wrongly gives his brother John Thomas Serres as 1783-1864 (correct: 1759-1825). Johannes Innevelt (no dates found; no known pictures). Fellow pupil of van Bergen under Adriaen van de Velde; known only from documents (Hofstede de Groot Vol. 4 p.469). Held back because there is no work to study - a study would be a study of a name in a document. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. New names from Kobell's own network -- verified NOT STUDIED, for a later round (NOT part of the 8 extra spares above): Carl Rottmann (11 January 1797, Handschuhsheim - 7 July 1850, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German landscape painter, most famous of the Rottmann family. First drawing lessons from his father Friedrich, who taught drawing at Heidelberg University; moved to Munich in 1821/1822, married Friederike von Sckell in 1824, and through her came to the attention of Crown Prince Ludwig, who sent him to Italy in 1826/1827 to sketch the 28 Italian landscapes in fresco for the Hofgarten arcades (completed 1833), then commissioned a Greek cycle in 1834 (23 large landscapes, now in the Neue Pinakothek). Court painter in 1841. His school included Karl Lindemann-Frommel, Karl Ludwig Seeger, August Loffler and August Seidel. WHY: the Munich landscapist who raised landscape to the height of history painting in fresco - the direct successor to Dillis's and Kobell's Munich. The doorway is: from Dillis's Munich, hop to the painter Ludwig sent to Italy to fresco a whole arcade of landscapes. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file (parent verified both). Peter von Cornelius (23 September 1783, Dusseldorf - 6 March 1867, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German painter, a main representative of the Nazarene movement. Drawing classes at the Dusseldorf Academy from age twelve; Rome 1811-1819 with the Lukasbund; summoned to Munich by Crown Prince Ludwig in 1819 to decorate the Glyptothek; director of the Dusseldorf Academy in 1821 and of the Munich Academy from 1824/1825; his "Last Judgment" (1829-1840) fills the east wall of the Ludwigskirche in Munich, 19 m by 12 m. His pupils included Karl Hermann, Wilhelm von Kaulbach and Adam Eberle; he left Munich for Berlin in 1839-1840. WHY: the academy director who rebuilt German painting around fresco and the Munich school - the institutional successor to Kobell's generation. The doorway is: from Kobell's Munich Academy, hop to the Nazarene who took its directorship and covered a church wall with the Last Judgment. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file (the file cornelius-cardew.md in the studies directory is Cornelius Cardew, a different person; parent verified both).



(The 14 names carried in #539 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam), Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. Dutch Rococo painter and interior decorator; pupil of Albert van Spiers and Jacob van Hal, Antwerp Guild of St Luke 1714; copied Rubens's ceilings in the Carolus Borromeuskerk in watercolour 1711-1722, the record after lightning destroyed them in 1718 (engraved by his pupil Jan Punt, 1751); famous for door and ceiling paintings in Amsterdam canal houses (he lived on the Keizersgracht) and country houses on the Vecht; grisailles he called "witjes" - grey-white paintings faking stone relief; Moses Choosing the Seventy Elders (1737, Amsterdam Town Hall), Apotheosis of Aeneas for Pieter Pels (Herengracht 479), ceiling with Isaac de Moucheron; pupils Jan Punt, Pieter Tanje, Dionys van Nijmegen, Jan de Groot, Frans and Jacob Xavery; follower Tako Hajo Jelgersma. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the Moucheron line's other half - the figure painter inside the wall-painter's room - and the witje is the one trompe-l'oeil mechanism this chain has never studied. The doorway is the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: one conservation page heads him "(1685-1754)" while its own body gives baptized 19 December 1695 - the 1685 is a typo. Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: teacher conflict - Houbraken says disciple of Adriaen van de Velde, RKD says pupil of David Colijns; death c.1695 (Wikipedia) vs 1666 (artsdot, wrong) vs 1690 or later (British Museum); birth c.1612 (Sphinx) vs c.1615 (Wikipedia/British Museum 1614/15). Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. FLAG: artsdot conflates father and son (its "Jacob Koninck (1615-1666)" page mixes the father's Copenhagen court career with the son's); myopenmuseum's "Jacob Coning (active c.1480s-1520s)" is a different, Flemish namesake and must not be cited for this man. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Marine painter; pupil of Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff; painted in an attic room on the Singel, probably employed by dealer Laurens Cornelisz de Coninck; follower and copyist of Ludolf Bakhuizen (1687/1689 notarial records of copying Bakhuizen); The Africa in Table Bay; most marines 1667-1678, later style tending to Bakhuizen; Amsterdam Guild of St Luke 1688; died aged about 70, buried at the Leiden cemetery. The doorway is the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who built his patron real ships. Marine painter; studied under Ludolf Bakhuizen; spent most of his life at the court of Frederick I of Prussia, returned to Amsterdam later in life, where he commissioned the building of two beautiful ships for Frederick I's pleasure; most of his paintings are in Germany. The doorway is the marine painter who built his patron real ships. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. The doorway is the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 - 7 November 1708; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left: clerk and calligrapher turned painter, student of Everdingen then Dubbels, who went out in an open boat to study storms and became the leading Dutch marine painter after 1672. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the pupil first, and Bakhuizen is the sea line, not the cattle line. The doorway is the clerk who went out in an open boat to study storms. He is main 3 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the pupil and the marine line, not through the menagerie. The doorway is the man who painted from live animals at Versailles. (The 14 names carried in #537 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jan Weenix (1642-1719), Frans Snyders (11 November 1579 - 19 August 1657), Paul de Vos (1591/92 or 1595 - 30 June 1678) (mains); Melchior d'Hondecoeter (c. 1636 - 3 April 1695) (reserve); Jan Hackaert (bapt. February 1628 - in/after 1685), Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707) (alternates); spares: Gerrit Berckheyde (bapt. 6 June 1638 - 10 June 1698), Pieter de Molijn (bapt. 6 April 1595 - 23 March 1661), Caesar van Everdingen (1616/17 - bur. 13 October 1678), Pieter de Grebber (c. 1600 - 1652/53), Willem Cornelisz Duyster (1599-1635), Salomon van Ruysdael (c. 1602 - bur. 3 November 1670), Willem van de Velde the Elder (c. 1611 - 13 December 1693), Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661). Jan Weenix (1642-1719; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the hunt line carried into dead-game still life. Trained by his father Jan Baptist Weenix (carried #531) with his cousin Melchior d'Hondecoeter; dead game and hunting scenes; Utrecht guild 1664 and 1668; painted Peter the Great 1697; court painter to Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm 1702-1712/14, 12 large hunting pictures for Schloss Bensberg; pupils his daughter Maria Weenix and Dirk Valkenburg (carried #536 extra spare); Goethe devoted a poem to his animal textures; the Wallace Collection holds 13 paintings. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the copy line runs through the cattle painter first, and Weenix is the still-life end of the hunt, not its living middle. The doorway is the hunter who became the still-life painter of dead game. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. FLAG: birth year uncertain - sources give between 1641 and 1649; 1642 is the commonly cited year; the page notes he married in 1679 "around thirty" and had 13 children 1680-1700. Frans Snyders (11 November 1579 - 19 August 1657; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Antwerp root of the whole hunt and animal line. Pupil of Pieter Brueghel the Younger 1593, then Hendrick van Balen; master 1602; Italy 1608-09 (Rome, Milan, Cardinal Borromeo); married Margaretha de Vos 1611 (sister of Cornelis and Paul de Vos); Rubens collaboration from the 1610s; Torre de la Parada 1636-38, about 60 hunting and animal pieces after Rubens designs, plus 18 more 1639; dean 1628; pupils Nicasius Bernaerts, Peter van Boucle, Juriaen Jacobsze, Jan Roos, Paul de Vos; Jan Fyt student and assistant from 1629; patrons Archduke Albert VII, Philip IV of Spain, Bishop Antonius Triest; influenced Jan Davidsz. de Heem and the 18th-century French animaliers Oudry and Desportes; the concert of birds theme; died childless. He is main 3 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the son's copying, not through Snyders directly, and his scale would swamp the Haarlem chain. The doorway is the Antwerp root of the hunt. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Paul de Vos (1591/92 or 1595 - 30 June 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's brother-in-law and workshop colleague; the second Antwerp hunt painter. Pupil of Denijs van Hove 1604, David Remeeus 1605, Eduard Snayers; worked in his brother-in-law Frans Snyders' workshop (Snyders married his sister Margaretha 1611); guild master 1620; monumental hunts for aristocrats, warmer colours and broader brush than Snyders, fighting cats, horses attacked by wolves; collaborator with Rubens (Torre de la Parada), Van Dyck, Jan Wildens; patrons Marquis of Leganes and Philippe-Charles 3rd Count of Arenberg (36+ paintings 1633-1640); many works long misattributed to Snyders; never dated his paintings. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is Snyders's shadow, and the study should meet Snyders first. The doorway is the brother-in-law in Snyders's shadow who painted the fighting cats. Reserve: Melchior d'Hondecoeter (c. 1636 - 3 April 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the bird painter: grandson of Gillis, son of Gijsbert, nephew of J. B. Weenix, cousin of Jan Weenix; trained by his father Gijsbert then his uncle J. B. Weenix; Confrerie Pictura 1659; married Susanne Tradel 1663; painted almost exclusively birds (exotic and game) in park landscapes; about 250 works, only about 20 dated; pupils Willem Hendrik Wilhelmus van Royen and Jan Weenix; employed by William III to paint his menagerie at Het Loo; his inventory lists a small gallows (to pose birds) and seven Snyders paintings; followers include Adriaen Coorte, Jan van Huysum, Elias Vonck; early sea-pieces and Tub with Fish 1655 before switching to birds. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the bird as still life in a park landscape, the menagerie as subject) is the most specialised in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine line: son of Willem the Elder, elder brother of Adriaen; trained by his father then Simon de Vlieger (Weesp); Amsterdam until 1672, then England with his father, Charles II service, studio at Queen's House Greenwich, Westminster 1691; leading Dutch marine painter of the later 17th century. Salomon van Ruysdael (c. 1602 - bur. 3 November 1670; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Guild Haarlem 1623; follower of Jan Porcellis and Esaias van de Velde; uncle and teacher of Jacob van Ruisdael (carried #534); father of Jacob Salomonsz van Ruysdael; brother Isaack van Ruisdael; tonal river landscapes; with van Goyen and de Molijn a pioneer of naturalistic Dutch landscape. His is the study of the tonal river painter who taught his nephew. The doorway is the uncle who taught Ruisdael the river. Willem van de Velde the Elder (c. 1611 - 13 December 1693; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - pen paintings (penschilderij) in black ink on vellum, panel and canvas; sailed with the fleet sketching battles (Scheveningen 1653, Sole Bay 1672, about 70 sketches); Charles II paid him 100 pounds a year for "draughts of sea fights" and the same to his son to colour them; father of Willem the Younger and Adriaen, and daughter Magdalena (b. 1632); Amsterdam studio on Nieuwe Waalseiland; commissions from Admiral Cornelis Tromp; died London 1693, buried St James's Piccadilly. His is the study of the father who drew sea battles in pen while his sons painted them in oil. The doorway is the father who sketched the fleet in pen. Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's pupil and assistant 1629-31, master 1629/30; Italy 1633-41 (Paris, Venice for the Sagredo and Contarini families, Rome 1635, Bentvueghels bent-name "Goudvink", bullfinch); back in Antwerp by 1641; Guild of Romanists dean 1652; married Francoise van de Sande 22 March 1654, four children; about 380 paintings (287 extant still lifes per Christie's); moved the dead-game display from the indoor table to the open landscape, the first to paint game as a trophy of the hunt rather than food; pupils Pieter Boel and Jacob van der Kerckhoven; collaborators Cornelius Schut, Thomas Willeboirts Bosschaert, Erasmus Quellinus II; Set of the Dogs 1642 dedicated to Don Carlo Guasco, Marquess of Soleno. His is the study of the man who took dead game out of the kitchen and into the landscape. The doorway is the pupil who moved the game from the table to the field. Philips Koninck (5 November 1619 - 4 October 1688; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Amsterdam; younger brother and pupil of Jacob Koninck; Houbraken says he completed his training in Rembrandt's studio (Britannica doubts it); from the late 1640s the panoramic landscapes, canvas split half earth and half sky, bands of shade and light after Rembrandt's etchings and Hercules Segers; figures by Adriaen van de Velde in Woodland border and countryside; about 300 drawings; owned a barge line Amsterdam-Rotterdam via Leiden; stopped painting after 1676. Jan van der Heyden (5 March 1637 - 28 March 1712; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Gorinchem-Amsterdam; one of the first Dutch painters to specialise in townscapes and a leading architectural painter of the Golden Age; his most frequent collaborator was Adriaen van de Velde; designed and implemented Amsterdam's street-lighting scheme (1669-1840, adopted abroad); married Sara ter Hiel 1661; 14 paintings connected with Maarssen, probably for Joan Huydecoper II; died wealthy, over 70 of his own paintings in his estate; Room Corner with Curiosities painted at 75 in 1712, the year he died. Simon de Vlieger (c. 1601 Rotterdam - bur. 13 March 1653 Weesp; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - marine painter; Delft Guild 1634; early work after Jan Porcellis (his teacher per the Royal Museums Greenwich), then his own silvery light and cool palette; teacher of Willem van de Velde the Younger (c. 1648-1650) and, per one low-quality source, of Adriaen van de Velde; the bridge between the second and third generations of Dutch marine painters; also influenced Hendrick Dubbels and Jan van de Cappelle. Jan Porcellis (1580/84 Ghent - 29 January 1632 Zoeterwoude; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - marine painter; initiated the decisive transition from early realism to the tonal phase: overcast skies, rough waters, low horizons, two-thirds of the canvas sea and sky; supposed pupil of Hendrick Vroom (per Houbraken); Antwerp guild 1617, Rotterdam by 1615, Haarlem around 1622; teacher of Simon de Vlieger per the Royal Museums Greenwich; the most influential marine painter of the first half of the 17th century; only about ten works securely identified. Hercules Segers (c. 1589 - c. 1638; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Haarlem-born; pupil of Gillis van Coninxloo; the most inspired, experimental and original landscapist of his period and an even more innovative printmaker; 54 plates, 183 known impressions, about 15 paintings; printed in coloured ink on coloured paper and cloth, hand-coloured, cropped to different sizes, each impression a miniature painting; invented lift-ground (sugar-bite) etching, not used again for 150 years; Rembrandt owned eight of his paintings and a printing plate; art dealer by trade; wife recorded as a widow in 1638.
